### 1. ★★★★☆ US-Ukraine-European negotiators meet in Miami as Trump announces diesel deal with Russia - Reuters

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: Reuters World / BBC World / BBC Business / GN Geopolitics / DW World
- **多源确认**: 9
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T00:03:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Figure caption, Watch: &#x27;Time for Ukraine to get a new president&#x27;, says Trump
US President Donald Trump has said he thinks "it&#x27;s time for Ukraine to get a new president" as he responded to Volodymyr Zelensky&#x27;s criticism of the US diesel deal with Russia.
Zelensky warned a new deal to release millions of tonnes of Russian diesel into the US would provide Moscow with money to continue its war in Ukraine, calling it a gift to Russian President Vladimir Putin.
Speaking as he left the White House on Saturday, Trump said the Ukrainian president "wants to make problems for the world" by continuing to authorise attacks on Russian refineries.
"He&#x27;d better damn well stop," Trump said before suggesting Ukraine should "get a new leader who can make a deal".
Russia and Ukraine have recently intensified their strikes, targeting energy-generating and transport facilities, as well as major logistics hubs.
"We said, &#x27;you can do whatever you want to Russia, don&#x27;t hit the refineries&#x27;, because that&#x27;s a world problem that&#x27;s caused," he said.
"He could have made many deals and for some reason, he never does."
The US president confirmed on Friday that an agreement had been reached with Moscow to suspend sanctions on Russian diesel exports until 7 April.
Zelensky reacted by saying it would allow Moscow to raise new funds to source new military equipment to continue the war.
He also accused Moscow of taking the deal "to mean they can keep fighting, to mean they keep dropping bombs on cities" following deadly new strikes on Zaporizhzhia.
Analysis: Trump&#x27;s shock Russia deal highlights mounting pressure to curb fuel prices
The Ukrainian president spoke to several allies on Saturday to discuss the latest developments, calling for "real decisions to protect lives in Ukraine" and to protect European security.
His criticism of the US-Russia diesel deal was echoed by EU&#x27;s foreign affairs chief Kaja Kallas echoed Zelensky&#x27;s criticism, saying suspending sanctions "provides Moscow with more revenues to wage war", adding: "This is not the time to ease pressure on Russia, and Europe won&#x27;t."
She said European foreign ministers intended to approve the biggest set of sanctions on Russia since the start of its full-scale invasion on Monday.
The deal with Russia is the latest effort from Trump to lower fuel prices in the US ahead of the midterm elections, after months spent grappling with the political consequences of the Iran war.
That conflict, which began in February, has sent the cost of petrol and in particular diesel skyrocketing, which has soured his standing with the American public who have been stung by the higher pump prices and knock-on effects that led to across-the-board inflation.
The average price of diesel is currently $6.28 (£4.74) a gallon, according to the AAA – up from last month&#x27;s $5.94 average, and $3.68 last year.
Under the deal, Russia would release an initial 300,000 tonnes of diesel "to the American and global marketplace" followed by an additional 500,000 tonnes in November.
A further million would follow, Trump wrote on Truth Social, and then another 3m tonnes "within a short period of time" – but he noted that this delivery will be "based on the condition of their diesel refineries".
In the past 10 months, Russia experienced two waves of severe fuel shortages across the country due to Ukrainian drones strikes on its oil refineries.
Diesel production in the Russia dropped by an estimated 30%, according to the International Energy Agency.
Trump had already called for new elections in Ukraine last December , external , saying Ukrainian voters should have the choice to replace Zelensky.
Since the war broke out in 2022 Ukraine has been under martial law, meaning elections are suspended.
Trump announces deal for Russian diesel as Zelensky calls it a gift to Putin
Russian glide bomb attack on Zaporizhzhia kills at least 15 people

### 3. ★★★★☆ Trump says Norway has 'indelible stain' for not awarding him Nobel Peace Prize - Reuters

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Reuters World / BBC World / GN Geopolitics / Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 4
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T01:32:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Image caption, The South African former ICC judge was awarded the prize on Friday
Navi Pillay, the winner of this year&#x27;s Nobel Peace Prize, has called US sanctions on the International Criminal Court (ICC) "unheard of" and "unacceptable".
The South African former ICC judge was awarded the prize on Friday "for her efforts to promote peace and international law", which paved the way for a "more comprehensive global legal order", the Nobel committee said.
US President Donald Trump expressed his disatisfaction with the pick and hours later the US unveiled sweeping sanctions in a significant acceleration of its attempt to "dismantle" what it described as a "rogue" institution.
The ICC said the measures were an assault on the rule of law and the foundations of the international legal order.
Pillay, speaking via video link during an event at the Nobel Peace Center in Oslo on Saturday, said: "There is so much tension and anxiety over the continued existence of international courts, mainly the International Criminal Court in The Hague.
"Most importantly, sanctions have been announced by one country against the judges, just for the judicial orders they issue. This is unheard of, unacceptable."
US Secretary of State Marco Rubio earlier said the sanctions - which include a ban on US entities making transactions with the ICC - were intended to "defend US sovereignty" and accused the court of seeking to prosecute Americans.
The US has already sanctioned 17 ICC judges and officials, but this move sanctions the court itself as an institution and is intended to inhibit its ability to operate financially.
However, the US has provided a six-month grace period before some of these latest sanctions kick in, meaning member states can keep paying for its operations - a move Washington says is to allow countries time to negotiate a "solution to address" US concerns.
The Trump administration claims that the ICC is run by what it calls globalists who threaten American sovereignty.
It is furious over a past investigation into actions of US soldiers in Afghanistan the court has since closed and by its arrest warrants for Israeli officials including Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu. The new sanctions, however, do not say that those warrants must be withdrawn before the measures can be lifted.
UN Secretary General Antonio Guterres called the US decision "a serious blow to our collective pursuit of international criminal justice, of which the ICC is a key pillar".
The UK joined Canada, Denmark, Germany, France, Italy, Japan and the Netherlands in saying they "regret and strongly disagree with" the sanctions.
Figure caption, Watch: Who is Nobel Peace Prize winner Navi Pillay?
Pillay, 85, also served as UN rights chief, taking part in high-profile work considering allegations of human rights violations, including in Rwanda, Myanmar and Gaza.
Her early work included defending anti-apartheid activists in South Africa, and on Friday she became the country&#x27;s fifth Peace Prize winner - following in the footsteps of Nelson Mandela and Archbishop Desmond Tutu.
"If you clap a little bit more I may share this prize with Trump," she joked after receiving the prize, a reference to last year&#x27;s winner, Venezuelan opposition figure María Corina Machado, deciding to gift her prize to the US president.
Trump called the announcement of Pillay as 2026&#x27;s winner "disgraceful and embarassing", and an "indelible stain on the country of Norway".
He has long indicated he thought he should have been awarded the prestigious prize, and was nominated by several allies for it last year.
US unveils sanctions on ICC in move court condemns as &#x27;assault on rule of law&#x27;
&#x27;Stain on the country&#x27;: Trump criticises Norway over Nobel Peace Prize

### 4. ★★★☆☆ The midterm strategy driving Democrats deep into Trump country - Reuters

- **分类**: 🇺🇸 美国
- **来源**: Reuters World / AP World / BBC World / BBC Business / GN Energy
- **多源确认**: 12
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T12:28:25+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Figure caption, How widely used is the death penalty in the US?
US death row inmate Christa Pike has been discharged from hospital following her botched execution at the end of last month, her lawyers have said.
Pike, 50, has been sent back to the prison where she has spent the last 30 years and is continuing to receive medical care, according to a statement from her lawyers on Saturday.
She survived two lethal injections of the drug pentobarbital on 30 September, which left her in a critical condition.
Pike regained consciousness earlier this week and began speaking - stunning experts, medical staff and her lawyers.
Her case is now in uncharted territory as she is thought to be the only person to have survived an execution attempt by lethal injection.
Pike&#x27;s lawyers said in their statement that she was "grateful to the first responders and her medical team for their care in these extraordinary circumstances".
It is unclear what medical care Pike is receiving now that she is back in prison but her lawyers previously said she faced a "long road to recovery".
The Tennessee Department of Correction is yet to say why the execution did not go as planned, though it has defended the method it used.
I&#x27;ve seen nearly 500 executions - but never one like Christa Pike&#x27;s
I interviewed Christa Pike 20 years ago - I can&#x27;t stop thinking about her
What happened in Christa Pike&#x27;s failed US execution, and how did she survive?
At a court hearing on Wednesday, Tennessee Assistant Attorney General Will Ayers told the judge he did not know whether the state intends to try to execute her again.
The judge ordered the state to preserve all evidence related to the failed injections. That evidence could lay the groundwork for a future lawsuit against the state.
Pike was 18 when she and her then-boyfriend, Tadaryl Shipp, beat, tortured and killed 19-year-old Colleen Slemmer, whom they had met at a job training camp for low-income young people.
Shipp was spared the death penalty because he was not legally an adult when the crime was committed. He remains in prison.
Pike was the first woman scheduled to be executed in the state of Tennessee in over 200 years.
Her lawyers have spent decades appealing her case. In the days leading up to her scheduled execution, both the US Supreme Court and Tennessee Governor Bill Lee declined to block the execution.
Since it failed, Pike&#x27;s lawyers have renewed their calls for her sentence to be commuted to life in prison without the possibility of parole.
Image caption, Colleen Slemmer was tortured and brutally killed by Pike and Shipp
Pike lost consciousness and was taken to a local hospital following her botched execution. Her legal team believed for days that she was brain dead and expected her to be taken off life support at any moment.
Pike eventually woke up and began talking again on Tuesday - her lawyer Randy Spivey said her first words were "Where am I?" and "Who are you?"
At a press conference on Wednesday, her lawyers said she was shackled to the bed and suffering from pneumonia and significant swelling on her arms, while also being unable to swallow.
"We don&#x27;t know the toll all of this will take" Spivey said of her mental health, adding that Pike felt "angry and confused".
Christa Pike now walking after failed US execution, lawyer tells BBC
Christa Pike has &#x27;profound&#x27; health turnaround after botched execution, lawyers say

### 5. ★★★☆☆ UAE says Omani flydubai co-pilot planned suicide attack on Tel Aviv flight - Reuters

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: Reuters World / BBC World / DW World / Guardian World / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 5
- **时间**: 2026-10-09T21:06:30+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Image caption, The Flydubai flight was diverted to Saudi Arabia after passengers managed to take back the aircraft&#x27;s controls
By Simon Atkinson ,  BBC Australia producer   and   Jaroslav Lukiv
The Omani co-pilot who allegedly attempted to hijack a Flydubai plane started a "drift towards extremism and terrorism" while studying in Australia, United Arab Emirates officials say.
UAE Attorney-General Hamad Saif al-Shamsi also alleged the pilot - widely named as Hamam al-Hammami - was last week trying to crash the plane into Tel Aviv&#x27;s airport in a "suicide attack".
He wounded the captain in the cockpit with the emergency crash axe before being overpowered by passengers. Another crew on the Dubai-Tel Aviv flight was able to land the aircraft in Saudi Arabia.
Melbourne university RMIT earlier confirmed the pilot had studied there but left before completing his engineering qualification.
What began as a routine flight from Dubai to Tel Aviv turned into a fight for survival when one pilot allegedly stabs another inside the cockpit.
According to Shamsi, interrogations of the 29-year-old pilot revealed he had been inspired by al-Qaeda&#x27;s 11 September attacks in the US in 2001.
"The findings indicated that signs of his drift towards extremism and terrorism first emerged in 2017, while he was studying in Australia," the attorney-general was quoted as saying by the UAE&#x27;s state news agency Wam on Friday.
"During that period, he was influenced by takfiri [radical] ideology through reading extremist literature and following online content glorifying terrorist organisations and suicide attacks."
The Wam report also said the pilot had told investigators "he had prepared himself both psychologically and physically to carry out the plot by intensifying his exposure to material promoting terrorism and training in boxing under a private coach".
"He admitted that he had acted alone, without the involvement of others, although investigations are continuing to verify this claim," Wam said.
The Australian Broadcasting Corporation has reported the man was issued an Australian student visa in 2014 and studied in the country in 2015-17, before leaving.
This week, the Sydney Morning Herald unearthed tweets from al-Mammami&#x27;s now-deleted X account in which he referred to his five years spent studying abroad in Australia, Britain, and New Zealand, as "the golden years of my religious commitment".
More than 170 passengers and crew were on board flight FZ1073 on 30 September.
In the days after the incident, Australian authorities confirmed they were looking into the man&#x27;s links to Australia.
The Victorian Joint Counter Terrorism Team - made up of the Australian Federal Police (AFP), Victoria Police and the country&#x27;s security agency ASIO - said it was "investigating the man&#x27;s links to Australia".
Figure caption, Watch: Tearful pilot recounts moment of Flydubai attack
Indian co-pilot Capt Smit Machchhar has been hailed a hero after managing to unlock the cockpit door while being violently attacked with an axe, allowing passengers to get in and take control of the aircraft.
Questions are also being asked about major security lapses, including how the attacker was recruited by Flydubai and given clearance to fly a plane filled with Israelis to Tel Aviv.
Regional officials said the co-pilot was barred from flying by Oman&#x27;s national carrier, Oman Air, and moved to an administrative job because of concerns about his extremist views.
He was later hired by UAE-based Flydubai after undergoing standard security checks that did not examine his background for potential signs of radicalisation, according to the officials.
Israeli aviation regulations also state that pilots who are citizens of countries without formal diplomatic relations with Israel, like Oman, are not permitted to operate flights to the country.
Flydubai co-pilot planned to crash plane into Tel Aviv airport or building, reports say
&#x27;I was not going to let others die&#x27; - pilot of Flydubai flight describes cockpit attack by co-pilot

### 6. ★★★☆☆ If a Stock Market Crash Is Coming, Warren Buffett Says This Is the Smartest Move to Make Right Now - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Markets
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-09T19:20:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: If a Stock Market Crash Is Coming, Warren Buffett Says This Is the Smartest Move to Make Right Now
The stock market has been on an extraordinary run over the last few years. Since the bear market bottomed out on Oct. 12, 2022, the S&P 500 (SNPINDEX: ^GSPC) has risen 118% while the Nasdaq Composite (NASDAQINDEX: ^IXIC) has rocketed 165%. The Dow Jones Industrial Average (DJINDICES: ^DJI) has also surged at an impressive rate. This rally roughly started with the beginning of the generative artificial intelligence (AI) revolution following ChatGPT 's public launch in November 2022.
AI has helped Nvidia become the world's most valuable company and fueled hundreds of billions of dollars in spending on data centers, semiconductors, and power infrastructure. Naturally, the enthusiasm around AI is creating uncomfortable comparisons with the dot-com bubble.
Missed Nvidia in 2009? This Rare Signal Is Flashing Again. In 2009, a "Double Down" signal flashed for a little-known chipmaker called Nvidia. For the first time in years, that same "Total Conviction" signal is flashing for a company 1/100th the size of Nvidia. Continue »
What if AI is a bubble and stocks crash? Warren Buffett spent nearly 60 years offering investors answers that sound almost too simple: the smartest thing you can do is stay invested through volatility and uncertainty.
Image source: Getty Images. Warren Buffett doesn't try to time the markets
During an interview with CNBC back in March 2009, Buffett acknowledged that he didn't know where the stock market would bottom. More importantly, he said he had "no idea" what the stock market would do tomorrow, next week, next month, or next year.
That's a sobering admission from arguably the world's most famous investor, but it explains the philosophy behind Berkshire Hathaway . Buffett never tried to predict whether stocks would rise by next Tuesday. Instead, he did his best to determine what a business is worth and whether owning it at today's price could produce attractive returns over many years.
Maybe AI stocks will crash in 2027. Or maybe they will rally for another five years. Investors who sell everything because they are convinced a crash is on the way will have another problem: they eventually need to decide when to get back in. Getting one timing decision right is difficult, but getting both right consistently is nearly impossible.
During the dot-com boom, the S&P 500 peaked at 1,527 on March 24, 2000. By Oct. 9, 2002, the index had fallen to 777 -- a brutal decline of roughly 49%. It didn't surpass its old record until May 30, 2007. The length of that recovery is enough to make buy-and-hold investing sound terrifying.
The S&P 500 now sits around 7,800. This means that investors who bought near the absolute peak of the dot-com bubble -- the seemingly worst possible moment -- still watched the index increase roughly fivefold.
Along the way, those investors endured the dot-com collapse, the global financial crisis, the Covid-19 crash, the 2022 bear market, inflation, wars, recessions, banking scares, and countless predictions of the next catastrophe .
The lesson here isn't that stock prices can't fall. It's that long-term investing doesn't require you to predict every crash on time. America's largest companies should continue generating profits, reinvesting capital, developing new products, and adapting to change. Over long periods, those fundamentals matter far more than whether you perfectly predict the next bear market.
The dot-com era is a good case study showing what can happen when investors abandon valuation and business fundamentals. Many speculative internet companies disappeared entirely after the market crashed. While an index eventually recovers, individual companies that go bankrupt do not. This is why building a diversified portfolio matters.
For those choosing individual stocks, Buffett's investment framework is useful: look for durable competitive advantages, strong balance sheets, capable management teams, and healthy cash generation that is used to reward shareholders through dividends or buybacks.
Ultimately, Buffett's approach isn't based on believing the stock market never crashes. It's based on accepting that downturns are inevitable and admitting that no one can consistently predict when they'll happen. Investors who remain diversified, avoid speculative momentum trades, and own high-quality businesses for decades at a time rather than days or weeks don't need to predict the next market-turning event. They just need patience to survive it.

### 7. ★★★☆☆ US says Chinese actions off Taiwan are 'deeply destabilising' - Reuters

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Reuters World / GN Geopolitics / GN China / DW World
- **多源确认**: 4
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T01:10:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Top Chinese US Expert: China Could Cut Purchases from the US by 10 Times the Value of US Arms Sales to Taiwan
Professor Wu Xinbo’s exclusive interview with Phoenix News
At their September summit, the Chinese and US presidents agreed to build a “constructive relationship of strategic stability based on respect, fairness and reciprocity.” This new understanding is gradually taking shape through arrangements in trade, artificial intelligence and security.
On September 28, China announced the outcomes of the eighth round of China-US economic and trade consultations. The arrangements for mechanisms such as the China-US Trade Council and Investment Council were further clarified, while the framework for reciprocal tariff reductions worth $30 billion was developed in greater detail. The two sides also agreed to establish an AI dialogue mechanism, with the next exchange planned for November. Their militaries agreed to work towards a memorandum of understanding on crisis communication and prevention as soon as possible.
Against this backdrop, can Washington’s cautious approach to Taiwan develop into more binding commitments? How will the “irreplaceability” of Chinese supply chains shape future China-US economic relations? Can cooperation on AI move beyond technological competition into applications and risk governance? And with the US midterm elections approaching, what new variables might emerge in Trump’s China policy?
In this exclusive interview, Phoenix Global Insight speaks with Professor Wu Xinbo, Dean of the Institute of International Studies and Director of the Center for American Studies at Fudan University.
1. The September China-US summit added “respect, fairness and reciprocity” to the concept of a “constructive relationship of strategic stability.” This formulation grew out of the principles of equality, respect and mutual benefit that guided earlier trade negotiations. Principles originally applied to economic relations are now being extended to the overall bilateral relationship, providing a clearer framework for cooperation, competition and the management of differences.
2. On Taiwan, Wu argues that China should raise the costs for the United States when Washington takes actions that undermine China’s interests. Whether the US adopts “strategic clarity” or “strategic ambiguity,” China should retain the strategic initiative.
3. On trade, China’s “irreplaceability” extends across the entire supply chain. For the United States, talking about “decoupling” or “de-risking” is much easier than actually achieving it. Wu expects US economic policy towards China to become more pragmatic and targeted, reducing dependence in areas considered sensitive to national security where alternatives are available, while maintaining trade in less sensitive sectors.
4. On AI, China and the United States have considerable room for cooperation beyond technological competition, including applications, commercialisation and risk governance. Wu sees the “zero-sum mentality” of American policymakers as the main obstacle. In AI safety, he argues, cooperation is not a matter of choice but a necessity. However, the involvement of numerous non-state actors makes AI risk governance particularly difficult.
5. On regional conflicts, Wu warns that US secondary sanctions targeting Chinese financial institutions could have the effect of a “financial nuclear bomb,” with consequences for the international position of the dollar. He believes Washington would have to weigh these risks carefully. He also sees opportunities for China-US cooperation on Iran, the Strait of Hormuz, Ukraine and the Korean Peninsula.
6. With the midterm elections approaching, Trump still needs a “success story” in his relationship with China. Progress on trade and other bilateral issues would allow him to present diplomatic achievements to American voters. However, changes in US domestic politics could increase pressure over values, Chinese investment and Taiwan.
Interview by: Song Dongze, Chief International Affairs Writer, Phoenix News
Where Did the Six Words “Respect, Fairness and Reciprocity” Come From?
Phoenix Global Insight: The latest China-US summit has expanded the meaning of a “constructive relationship of strategic stability” by adding the principles of “respect, fairness and reciprocity.” When the two presidents agreed in May to build a constructive relationship of strategic stability, you described it as a shift from “tactical stability” to “strategic stability.” How do you assess this latest development? What does it mean for China-US relations going forward?
Wu Xinbo: The principles of “respect, fairness and reciprocity” come from the six Chinese characters meaning “equality, respect and mutual benefit” that were put forward during the China-US trade negotiations. China essentially conducted those negotiations in accordance with these principles. That is why the talks made progress and why the economic relationship stabilised. Now, however, the two sides need to stabilise not only their economic relationship but the entire China-US relationship. During Trump’s visit to China in May, the two countries reached an understanding on building a constructive relationship of strategic stability. The logic behind the latest formulation is the same. If equality, respect and mutual benefit are necessary to stabilise economic relations, then the same principles should apply to the broader bilateral relationship. There has been a slight adjustment in the wording. “Equality, respect and mutual benefit” has become “respect, fairness and reciprocity.” After the May meeting in Beijing, the White House actually used the terms “fairness” and “reciprocity” in its statement. These terms were consistent with the principles China had advocated during the negotiations. The English word “reciprocity” can be translated into Chinese as either “对等” or “互惠.” The nuances are slightly different, but the underlying meaning is broadly similar. So I believe the new six-character formulation draws directly on the principles China followed during the trade negotiations. After discussions between the two sides, these principles have now been extended to the overall China-US relationship in pursuit of strategic stability. We should also recognise that the United States attaches considerable importance to such concepts and formulations. During his welcoming remarks at the White House, Trump specifically mentioned that he and President Xi had established a “great friendship based on mutual respect and the fundamental interests of the two peoples.” So the American side is also talking about mutual respect and the fundamental interests of both peoples. That naturally brings in questions of fairness and reciprocity. These six words therefore reflect the latest China-US understanding on building a constructive relationship of strategic stability.
Phoenix Global Insight: You previously argued that, under the framework of constructive strategic stability, Washington has adopted a more cautious approach to Taiwan than in the past, which has helped maintain overall stability. With the addition of “respect, fairness and reciprocity,” do you think this American caution over Taiwan could develop into more binding policy commitments?
Wu Xinbo: I think the United States must move in that direction, for two reasons. The first is not really about the principles themselves, because principles alone cannot constrain the Americans. What matters more is that President Xi clearly explained China’s position on Taiwan during his visit to the United States. He made three main points. First, China’s position on safeguarding national reunification and territorial integrity is very clear. We are pursuing national reunification. Second, China hopes the United States will maintain what we regard as the correct position of opposing “Taiwan independence.” Since Trump’s visit to China, he has been relatively cautious in handling the Taiwan issue. Third, properly handling Taiwan is a solid foundation for China-US strategic cooperation and the development of bilateral relations. In other words, if the Taiwan issue is not handled properly, there can be no strategic stability between China and the United States, nor can bilateral relations and cooperation develop. That is a clear message about the consequences. The second reason is that China has cards to play. If the United States retreats from its position on Taiwan and damages China’s core interests, it will suffer substantial losses in its relationship with China. So when we talk about “respect” in the phrase “respect, fairness and reciprocity,” the first requirement is respect for China’s core interests, especially on Taiwan. Before President Xi’s visit to the United States, Ambassador Xie Feng published an article in People’s Daily discussing China’s four red lines in China-US relations. President Xi had previously raised these with Biden as well. They concern Taiwan, democracy and human rights, China’s political system and development path, and China’s right to development. I therefore believe these six words provide a clearer framework for how China and the United States should cooperate, the limits of their competition, and how their differences should be managed. They cover all these dimensions.
Whether Washington Chooses “Strategic Ambiguity” or “Strategic Clarity,” China Holds the Initiative
Phoenix Global Insight: Let us turn to specific issues concerning the Taiwan Strait. US arms sales to Taiwan have long been an important factor affecting China-US relations. Around Trump’s visit to China in May, he described a proposed arms sale to Taiwan worth approximately $14 billion as a potential bargaining chip in negotiations with China. US officials subsequently claimed that the sale would not depend on China’s actions. This once again highlights the difficulty of resolving the issue. Diplomatic protests have often failed to prevent Washington from continuing arms sales. How should China respond to prevent the United States from treating arms sales to Taiwan as bargaining chips?
Wu Xinbo: In the past, our responses have generally included diplomatic protests, sanctions against certain American companies and individuals, and military exercises around the Taiwan Strait to exert pressure. But it now appears that these measures have not been strong enough. They have not imposed costs on the United States that it genuinely feels. I think we need to increase the economic and security costs for the United States when it takes such actions. For example, if the United States sells $1 billion or $2 billion worth of weapons to Taiwan, China could cancel procurement contracts with American companies worth two or five times the value of those arms sales. That would impose a real economic cost on the United States.
Phoenix Global Insight: Beyond arms sales, the broader direction of US strategy towards Taiwan has also attracted considerable attention. In recent years, some voices in the United States have called for abandoning so-called “strategic ambiguity.” Meanwhile, Washington has continued arms sales to Taiwan, promoted joint development of unmanned systems through the National Defense Authorization Act, deepened military technology cooperation, and considered expanding Taiwan’s participation in regional military exchanges. Does this suggest that the United States is gradually abandoning strategic ambiguity? What implications could such a shift have for the Taiwan Strait?
Wu Xinbo: I don’t think we should be overly concerned about whether the United States adopts strategic clarity or strategic ambiguity. We should not allow ourselves to be led around by Washington. The key to the Taiwan issue is that China must retain the initiative and control over its own decisions. What does that mean? It means having sufficient strength and determination to handle and resolve the Taiwan issue in the way we consider most appropriate. The United States should have to adjust its policies according to China’s choices, rather than China’s Taiwan policy being determined by whether Washington chooses strategic clarity or ambiguity. Look at Biden. During his four years in office, he stated on four occasions that the United States would defend Taiwan if a conflict broke out. That sounded like strategic clarity. Trump no longer says that. He has returned to strategic ambiguity. But Biden’s strategic clarity was not necessarily all that clear, and Trump’s strategic ambiguity may not be all that ambiguous. What do I mean? Biden said the United States would defend Taiwan. But if a conflict actually broke out, would Washington really intervene? Could it win? Could it afford the costs? Trump, meanwhile, refuses to say whether the United States would intervene. But I believe he understands very well that American intervention in a cross-strait conflict would have little prospect of success and would come at an enormous cost. So I don’t think we should pay too much attention to whether Washington calls its policy clear or ambiguous. The important thing is that the Taiwan issue remains in China’s hands. We must retain the initiative.
Phoenix Global Insight: Taiwan’s semiconductor industry is also becoming an increasingly important factor in China-US competition. On September 2, Taiwan announced that local companies planned to invest an additional $20 billion in the United States. Previously, TSMC had expanded its planned US investment to $265 billion. Under pressure from Washington, Taiwan’s advanced semiconductor industry is becoming more deeply integrated into US supply chains. The United States is deliberately linking technological competition with the Taiwan issue. How might this affect the Taiwan Strait and China-US relations?
Wu Xinbo: I think the American motivation is quite straightforward. The United States is worried that a conflict in the Taiwan Strait could disrupt the reliability of its semiconductor supplies. So its main concern is supply security. In effect, Washington is saying: you cannot simply manufacture chips in Taiwan and sell them to us. You need to manufacture them in the United States so that we can feel secure. Now, Taiwan may believe that by cooperating with Washington, building factories in the United States and addressing American supply-chain concerns, it is offering the United States a valuable gift. Taiwan may think this will bring it closer to Washington and encourage the United States to provide stronger support. But I think the outcome could be exactly the opposite. Traditionally, the United States has valued Taiwan partly for geopolitical reasons. But today, particularly for Trump, the main concern is advanced semiconductors. If Taiwanese companies can produce enough advanced chips in the United States to meet American demand, then Washington no longer needs to worry so much about risks in the Taiwan Strait. That would substantially reduce Taiwan’s value to the United States. Taiwan originally hoped that investing in semiconductor manufacturing in America would secure greater American protection. But the result could be that Taiwan actually reduces its own strategic value. Trump might simply think: as long as my chip supply is secure, who cares what happens to Taiwan? So Taiwan could end up making a costly miscalculation. It may try to gain an advantage but end up worse off.
Decoupling Is Easy to Talk About but Difficult to Achieve: China’s “Irreplaceability” Goes Far Beyond Expectations
Phoenix Global Insight: Moving from semiconductors to the broader China-US economic relationship, you previously argued that a country’s resilience in economic competition cannot be measured by trade volumes alone. We also need to consider how difficult it is to replace that country in critical sectors. Where do you think China’s “irreplaceability” is most evident today?
Wu Xinbo: I think China’s irreplaceability can be found in many areas, across almost the entire supply chain, from upstream to midstream and downstream industries. At the upstream level, people immediately think of rare earths, critical minerals and active pharmaceutical ingredients. These are areas where the United States depends on Chinese supply chains. But there are also large numbers of intermediate products that the United States cannot do without. Take America’s AI infrastructure expansion. The United States is building thousands of data centres. Those facilities require all kinds of computer equipment and electrical systems, many of which have to be imported from China. So the United States remains dependent on China. The same is true in advanced manufacturing, including robotics and drones. I would say China’s irreplaceability goes far beyond what many of us imagine. And the United States is becoming increasingly aware of this reality, sometimes painfully so.
Phoenix Global Insight: Despite Washington’s efforts to remove China from its supply chains, bilateral trade has not moved towards complete decoupling. How do you think this situation will evolve? What changes might we see in the economic relationship?
Wu Xinbo: I think slogans such as decoupling and de-risking are much easier to proclaim than to implement. As I just explained, the United States is discovering that it depends on China across almost every part of the supply chain. So it cannot completely decouple, nor can it fully remove China from its supply chains. I therefore expect future US economic policy towards China to become increasingly pragmatic. Washington will need to redefine the boundaries of its economic relationship with China. What does that mean? There will be areas where the United States believes it must reduce dependence on China for so-called national security reasons, and where it actually has the ability to do so. There will be other areas where Washington wants to reduce dependence but cannot realistically achieve it. In those cases, it will have to compromise and find some kind of middle ground. And there will be non-sensitive sectors, unrelated to military conflict, where decoupling is unnecessary and the risks are limited. Trade and economic cooperation can continue in those areas. So I think US economic policy towards China will have to become more pragmatic and more precisely targeted. It will need to establish new boundaries for the relationship.
Phoenix Global Insight: So China-US economic relations may evolve into a state of partial decoupling?
Wu Xinbo: During the Biden administration, the US Secretary of Commerce visited China and made a remark in Shanghai. She said that only about 1% of China-US trade was problematic, while the other 99% was not. So if decoupling is necessary, perhaps only 1% truly needs to be decoupled, and only in areas where the United States can actually achieve it. The remaining 98% or 99% does not need to be decoupled. That gives us a clearer boundary and helps redefine how the two countries should manage their economic relationship. This is extremely important for the stability and predictability of China-US trade.
The Biggest Obstacle to China-US AI Cooperation Is America’s “Zero-Sum Mentality”
Phoenix Global Insight: Beyond trade, technology has become increasingly important in China-US relations. Competition over advanced semiconductors and AI continues to intensify. The repeated adjustments to US export policies for Nvidia AI chips illustrate Washington’s dilemma: it wants to restrict China’s access to advanced computing power, but it cannot completely ignore the commercial interests of American companies. As technological competition intensifies, is there still room for limited China-US cooperation on AI? Or will broader strategic rivalry make meaningful cooperation increasingly difficult?
Wu Xinbo: If we look at the situation objectively, China and the United States are already cooperating in AI, and they need to cooperate more in the future. The problem is that American policymakers largely approach the development of the Chinese and American AI industries with a zero-sum mentality. They believe that the United States must not only maintain its technological lead but also do everything possible to suppress China and slow down the development of its AI industry, both technologically and commercially. As a result, the cooperation that already exists between the two countries has not received the recognition it deserves, and the potential for further cooperation has not been fully realised. If we look at the possibilities for cooperation, AI can broadly be divided into three areas: technological development, applications and commercialisation, and governance and risk management. China and the United States are already cooperating in all three areas. Take technological development. The United States is ahead in AI technology, but China has also made important contributions. For example, more and more American AI companies are using models developed by Chinese AI companies. Many of these models are open-source and relatively inexpensive, so an increasing number of American businesses are choosing to use them. On the application side, I think we will see even more opportunities for cooperation. China has moved quickly in applying AI, particularly in robotics and advanced manufacturing. These are areas where China has strengths that the United States needs. Then there is governance. The development and application of AI will bring many risks. As two of the world’s leading countries in AI, China and the United States must discuss what risks these technologies create, how to identify them, how to manage them, and how to cooperate in addressing them. So there is actually enormous room for China-US cooperation in AI. The main problem is that American policymakers are too narrow-minded. They constantly think that maintaining America’s lead requires suppressing China’s development. As a result, they ignore both the reality of existing cooperation and the need for further cooperation. That, in my view, is the biggest obstacle.
Phoenix Global Insight: You just mentioned that China and the United States have room to cooperate in preventing AI risks. Some observers believe the two countries already share certain concerns about extreme AI risks. For example, neither wants powerful AI systems to be misused by terrorist organisations or other malicious actors. Could preventing such risks provide an entry point for China-US cooperation on AI safety?
Wu Xinbo: I think addressing AI risks is precisely where China and the United States need to cooperate. This is not a question of whether they want to cooperate. They have to cooperate. AI development is bringing many disruptive challenges, and humanity has not yet fully understood the risks involved. As more potential risks emerge, the issue will require increasing attention from the international community, especially China and the United States. AI risks also have some unique characteristics. The actors involved may not be states at all. They could be individuals, organisations or companies. That makes the risks much harder to control, because there are simply too many non-state actors. And AI is still at a very early stage of widespread application. We do not yet know how many different kinds of harm, including potentially disruptive or catastrophic consequences, could emerge in the future. There is still an enormous amount of work to be done. That is why governing AI risks will be an extremely complex and difficult task.
The US Must Bear the Consequences of Secondary Sanctions Against Iran; Regional Issues Could Offer Opportunities for China-US Cooperation
Phoenix Global Insight: Looking at the broader international situation, regional conflicts such as the Russia-Ukraine war and the US-Iran conflict are also affecting China-US relations. On August 24, the United States announced an “economic isolation campaign” against Iran, introducing secondary sanctions that could affect third-party markets, including mainland China and Hong Kong. At the same time, US sanctions against Russia continue to affect normal economic and trade relations between China and Russia. For China, this means that even ordinary commercial activities face growing exposure to secondary sanctions. How should China protect its legitimate economic relationships without becoming constrained by the US sanctions system? And how might the spillover effects of these sanctions reshape China’s broader external economic strategy?
Wu Xinbo: Let’s take the US-Iran conflict as an example. The United States wants to intensify sanctions and has even threatened to take action against China. I think China’s position should have two elements. First, we should oppose American sanctions and make clear to Washington that these measures are unjustified and have no basis in international law. On Iran, during the Obama administration, an agreement was reached with the support of China and other countries. It was the United States that subsequently withdrew from that agreement unilaterally, violating international law. Then, last year and again this year, the United States unilaterally launched military attacks against Iran. So, in my view, the United States has violated international law and acted as an aggressor. It is neither legally nor morally justified in its approach to Iran. Under these circumstances, its sanctions are unreasonable and cannot be accepted by the international community. The second issue concerns the consequences if Washington targets Chinese financial institutions or major state-owned banks. We need to explain very clearly to the United States what such actions would mean. China is the world’s largest trading nation. A large share of our international trade is settled in US dollars, and we also hold hundreds of billions of dollars in US debt securities. China therefore plays an important role in supporting the international position of the dollar and the market for US government debt. If the United States sanctions Chinese banks, that would be like detonating a financial nuclear bomb. And if the result is an acceleration of China’s move away from the dollar, the United States will have to bear the consequences. China is a major economic, trading and financial power. If an economy of China’s size begins moving away from the dollar, Washington had better think carefully about the implications. We need to make these consequences clear to the Americans. As I said earlier, Americans know how to calculate costs and benefits. They understand what is at stake.
Phoenix Global Insight: Beyond the economic consequences of sanctions, regional conflicts such as the Russia-Ukraine war and the US-Iran conflict continue to affect global security. Although China and the United States disagree on some of these issues, they still share interests in preventing conflicts from spiralling out of control and maintaining energy and shipping security. Could the two countries cooperate in practical ways on these issues?
Wu Xinbo: I think the most urgent issue right now is the US-Iran conflict and the situation in the Middle East. The question of passage through the Strait of Hormuz affects global energy supplies and the direction of the world economy. Is there room for China-US cooperation here? Of course there is. For China, reopening the Strait of Hormuz and restoring freedom of navigation serve our interests, as well as those of the overwhelming majority of countries around the world. So we want freedom of navigation through the Strait to be restored as soon as possible. On the one hand, China can work with Iran. We should make clear that the Strait of Hormuz must not be held hostage to the US-Iran conflict. It is an international waterway, and disruptions there have consequences far beyond the countries directly involved. On the other hand, we also need to work with the United States. We should tell Washington that launching unilateral military attacks against Iran was wrong, that it needs to acknowledge its mistake and correct its policies. That means not launching further attacks and not continuing economic sanctions. If compensation is necessary, it should provide compensation. If guarantees against future military action are needed, it should provide those guarantees. Only then can Iran develop sufficient trust in the United States to reach a new agreement. So I think China should engage with both sides. We need to work with Iran, but even more importantly, we need to work with the United States. That is how China can play a constructive role.
Phoenix Global Insight: Apart from Iran, which other regional issues might provide opportunities for limited China-US cooperation?
Wu Xinbo: I think the Russia-Ukraine conflict and the Korean Peninsula are both areas where China and the United States could cooperate. On Ukraine, Trump has launched another round of diplomatic efforts, and the parties involved in the negotiations have sent some positive signals. If the directly involved parties become willing to sit down and negotiate, China could work with other countries to play a constructive role. Any eventual settlement of the Russia-Ukraine conflict is unlikely to be purely bilateral. It would probably need to be a multilateral agreement, with the United States, China and other major countries potentially acting as guarantors. In that sense, it could resemble the Iran nuclear agreement reached during the Obama administration. China could certainly make a constructive contribution to such a process. The Korean Peninsula is another example. We can see that South Korea’s Lee Jae-myung administration wants to improve inter-Korean relations and advance the peace process on the peninsula. Trump has also repeatedly expressed interest in meeting the North Korean leader to discuss improving US-North Korea relations. China could use its friendly relations with North Korea to play a positive and constructive role, including facilitating communication and offering proposals for the parties to consider. So I believe there are many international and regional issues on which China and the United States could cooperate. In the past, particularly during Trump’s first term, his foreign policy agenda was rather narrow. When it came to China, he was mainly interested in trade. In his second term, fentanyl was added to the agenda. But now it appears there are more issues than that. With the Iran situation, the United States seems to want China’s help in resolving problems. China could also play a role in Ukraine. As two major powers, China and the United States naturally have extensive opportunities for cooperation in international and regional affairs. If we can continue expanding cooperation on third-party issues through leader-level diplomacy, we will broaden the areas of cooperation in the bilateral relationship. That would create additional positive momentum for the stability and development of China-US relations.
With the Midterm Elections Approaching, Trump Wants to Turn China-US Relations into a Foreign Policy Success Story
Phoenix Global Insight: Finally, let’s turn from regional conflicts back to American domestic politics. The United States will hold its midterm elections this November. As the elections approach, how much room does Trump still have to adjust his China policy and negotiate with Beijing?
Wu Xinbo: I think Trump still depends heavily on presenting his China policy as a success. What he wants to say is: look how well I have handled the relationship with China. This is one of my foreign policy achievements. So he needs not only to stabilise the relationship but also to demonstrate progress. For example, achievements in trade or cooperation on drug enforcement are things he can present to American voters as evidence of his success.
Phoenix Global Insight: If the Democrats regain control of Congress after the midterm elections, how much could that affect Trump’s China policy during the second half of his second term? And looking further ahead, how might it shape US policy towards China after Trump leaves office?
Wu Xinbo: If the Democrats take control of the House of Representatives, I think the impact on China-US relations could be felt in three main areas. The first concerns values. Democrats may criticise Trump for treating relations with China primarily as a matter of doing business, while paying insufficient attention to human rights, democracy and values. They may argue that these issues must be given greater priority. So they could play the values card, creating additional political pressure. The second area concerns Chinese investment in the United States. Trump may consider using the proposed China-US Investment Council to encourage more Chinese investment in America. From his perspective, this would be good for the US economy. Chinese investment could create jobs and generate tax revenue. But Democrats, partly because of their ties to organised labour, may be concerned about Chinese investment, particularly in the American automotive industry. We have already seen Democratic lawmakers write to Trump urging him not to open the US automobile market to Chinese manufacturers. During the Biden administration, the United States imposed 100% tariffs on Chinese electric vehicles to prevent them from entering the American market. Democrats may therefore demand much stricter scrutiny and restrictions on Chinese investment. That is the second possible impact. The third area is Taiwan. Since the May summit between the Chinese and American presidents, Trump has generally handled Taiwan rather cautiously, including on arms sales. If the Democrats return to power in Congress, they may argue that improving relations with mainland China must not come at Taiwan’s expense. They may invoke the Taiwan Relations Act and argue that the United States is obligated to provide Taiwan with weapons. They could therefore press Trump to approve further arms sales. That would also affect how the Taiwan issue is handled. There may be other areas of impact, but I think these three would be the main ones.
Phoenix Global Insight: How much influence can Congress actually exert over Trump? After all, his willingness to disregard Congress seems almost unprecedented.
Wu Xinbo: Foreign policy is largely the responsibility of the executive branch, particularly the president. Congress can make noise and apply pressure, but ultimately Trump makes the decisions. What concerns me is the possibility that, after the midterm elections, the Democrats return to power and intensify their political confrontation with Trump. That could make Trump even more dependent on support from his Republican colleagues in Congress. And there are quite a few pro-Taiwan figures within the Republican Party. They might say to him: “Mr President, we can support you, but you need to sell Taiwan some weapons. It has been quite a while since the last major sale.” Trump may not necessarily listen to what Democrats say. But when Republicans make such demands, he may find it much harder to ignore them. That is one possible scenario. In other words, pressure from the Democrats could ultimately be transmitted through the Republican Party.
Phoenix Global Insight: Thank you, Professor Wu Xinbo, for this in-depth discussion, which has helped clarify the dynamics of China-US competition and cooperation across the Taiwan Strait, trade, technology and global governance.

Vessels transit the Strait of Hormuz off the coast of the port city of Bandar Abbas in southern Iran on September 5, 2026; illustrative

The Guards did not specify the tanker&#x27;s name or flag.

### 9. ★★★☆☆ Trump slams Zelenskyy, says Ukraine should elect new president

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: DW World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T17:48:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The US president has also warned Kyiv against targeting Russian oil refineries Image: Julia Demaree Nikhinson/AP Photo/picture alliance Advertisement
US President Donald Trump on Saturday said it's time for Ukraine to get a new president, suggesting that current Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy has not done enough to put an end to Ukraine's war with Russia.
Speaking at the White House, Trump said, "I suggest they get a new leader who can make a [peace] deal,"&#160;adding that ”for some reason [Zelenskyy]&#160;never does."
Speaking to Ukrainian citizens, Trump claimed Zelenskyy has "lost 30% of your country and you've had over a million people go between the two of them, over 2 million people killed. That's a war that should have never started. And I think it's time for Ukraine to get a new president."
Independent estimates put the percentage of Ukrainian territory under Russian control at 20%, with British defense officials recently stating that about half a million Russian soldiers had been killed in the war and that&#160;there were no&#160;reliable recent estimates about how many Ukrainians had died.
The US president also warned Kyiv against targeting Russian oil refineries.
Trump said he had told Zelenskyy to "do what you want" against Russia, but "don't hit refineries."

### 10. ★★★☆☆ Trump and Iran agree on one thing. The US election calendar isn't driving their decisions on the war - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics / DW World
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T04:32:57+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Trump and Iran agree on one thing. The US election calendar isn't driving their decisions on the war
President Donald Trump and Iranian officials seem to agree on one thing about their seven-month-old war
By AAMER MADHANI Associated Press and AMIR-HUSSEIN RADJY Associated Press
WASHINGTON -- President Donald Trump and Iran&#x27;s leadership appear to agree on one thing about their seven-month-old war : they each insist the U.S. election calendar isn&#x27;t motivating them to find an end to it.
Persistent inflation and high gas prices fueled by the conflict have driven widespread American disapproval of the war — a sentiment casting a long shadow over the Nov. 3 election that will determine whether Trump&#x27;s party keeps control of Congress. And U.S. economic sanctions and a naval blockade on Iranian oil are causing profound shocks for Tehran&#x27;s economy.
Yet, Iranian officials remain insistent there won&#x27;t be a deal until the U.S. lifts the blockade and delivers sanctions relief. Trump, meanwhile, is vacillating. He has dispatched more military resources to the region and threatened to resume strikes — even as he also declares that the U.S. “will not be attacking Iran at any time prior” to Nov. 3.
Speaking to fellow world leaders last month at the U.N. General Assembly, Trump insisted he “will not give credence to the election when it comes to Iran.”
Now, more than a week into a 32-day campaign blitz for Republican candidates, Trump is doubling down on that message, telling voters he&#x27;s winning the war while darkly warning he&#x27;s ready to "finish it off" if Iran doesn&#x27;t capitulate.
“I will tell you what: Iran will be very quick one way or the other,” Trump said Friday as he campaigned in New York. “Either they give us everything, or they don’t exist any longer.”
Trump&#x27;s campaign bravado comes after his stated war goals in Iran have shifted significantly over the course of the conflict.
He&#x27;s moved from maximalist demands for unconditional surrender by the theocratic government to a focus on economic pressure, containing Tehran&#x27;s nuclear program and reopening the Strait of Hormuz.
Iran&#x27;s economy is certainly in shambles. Its navy and air force have been devastated, and key political and military leaders were killed in the first weeks of the conflict.
Yet, Iran has shown no small measure of resilience in the face of Trump&#x27;s repeatedly being on the cusp of ordering big new strikes — only to back down at the last moment.
After months of grinding war, an American election season isn&#x27;t fundamentally changing Iran&#x27;s approach with the U.S. leader they&#x27;ve found to be an unreliable narrator, said Sasan Karimi, a former Iranian government official.
Iran&#x27;s leadership, Karimi added, is concerned that “the U.S., especially in the Trump era, will not be compliant to any deal even if he himself signs it.”
Trump&#x27;s message isn&#x27;t easy for Republican candidates to embrace as they make their case to voters frustrated by elevated gas prices and food costs.
Most Americans, 69%, say the war has not been worth fighting , according to a poll published last week by the Associated Press-NORC Center for Public Affairs Research . That&#x27;s up slightly from 64% in July. Only 28% approve of how Trump is handling Iran.
Some Republicans in competitive races have sought to distance themselves from Trump on the war.
In Michigan, Republican Senate candidate Mike Rogers has said the “war with Iran needs to end — and end quickly.” Rep. Ashley Hinson, who is in a competitive Senate race in Iowa, said Iowans shouldn’t have to “foot the bill” for the war. Seven House Republicans voted in favor of a war powers resolution aimed at curtailing Trump&#x27;s war of choice.
Yet, trying to stand apart from Trump on Iran is almost impossible for Republicans so close to Election Day, says Christopher Borick, director of the Lehigh University Opinion Observatory in Pennsylvania.
“It&#x27;s an enormous weight on Republicans,” Borick said. “Shifting the narrative to draw separation from Trump is going to be a Herculean task.”
Negotiations had appeared at a standstill after Trump late last month publicly rejected Iran’s proposal to quickly reopen the Strait of Hormuz if the U.S. met certain conditions.
Boosting US oil supplies with Russian diesel unlikely to have much impact on prices, experts say
In Silicon Valley, AI founders cheer Trump’s calls for the industry to police itself on safety
Florida execution called off after DeSantis withdraws death warrant for the first time
Iranians, however, say they have since received a U.S. counterproposal and Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi on Thursday said he expected Tehran would offer a formal reply within days. Trump on Thursday described the indirect negotiations as “productive.”
Iranian President Masoud Pezeshkian has said Tehran wants a return to the framework of the short-lived June deal. That agreement held out the promise of financial relief for Iran, which never materialized.
But the Iranian president also expressed skepticism. “We are trying to move the process forward. We are having conversations; every time we have held talks with them, they attacked us again," Pezeshkian said Thursday as he met with Russian President Vladimir Putin in Turkmenistan.
As part of any deal, Iranian officials say the U.S. must lift its blockade before Iran agrees to cease attacks on international oil tankers using the Strait of Hormuz .
“We are telling America, you have to act first. Enough talking, enough negotiations,” Mohsen Rezaei, a former Revolutionary Guard commander and secretary of Iran’s top security body, recently told Iranian state television, boasting that Iran had the upper hand in the conflict.
Some Iranian officials seem to believe that a return to full-scale war may be inevitable.
Mahdi Mohammadi, a Tehran-based analyst and an adviser to Parliament Speaker Mohammad Bagher Qalibaf , said on Telegram that while indirect talks with the U.S. have been “serious," he predicted that “the war will start" again soon.
At his rallies, Trump has pointed to a recent surge in Gulf oil making it through the Strait of Hormuz as he&#x27;s made his argument that Iran&#x27;s leadership will soon have to capitulate.
Indeed, Iran has lost its near-chokehold over oil flows through the waterway as U.S. Central Command shepherds oil tankers through the strait at night with their location systems turned off.
But in recent days, Iran has ramped up attacks on tankers passing through the strait. And overall, ship traffic remains well below prewar levels.
Tanker operators willing to risk running the gauntlet under Central Command’s protection are in some cases charging rates of $1 million a day or more, shipping analysts say.
The situation has become further complicated as Saudi Arabia and Yemen’s government have launched a military campaign aimed at driving Iran-backed Houthi rebels from the Bab el-Mandeb strait , another critical Middle East trade route for oil and other goods.
The Houthis, in turn, are targeting Saudi airports and key infrastructure in the kingdom with drone and missile strikes.
Trump insists that the U.S. naval blockade to prevent Iranian oil from getting to market has driven Tehran to the brink.
Iran’s oil minister Mohsen Paknejad recently resigned, Iranian state television announced this week. And the Iranian currency, the rial, continues to crater.
Iranian leaders appear convinced that they can still absorb military pressure, and impose further costs on the United States and the global economy, said Karimi, the former Iranian government official.
Hamzeh Safavi, a political scientist at the University of Tehran, said Iranian officials are giving little weight to the midterms as they calculate their next move.
“Iran knows that whether it’s Democrats or Republicans, there is a shared view about the fundamental confrontation with Iran," Safavi said. "They just might not agree regarding the method of confronting Iran. For that reason, the results won’t really create a foundational change in relations with Iran.”

### 11. ★★★☆☆ In three years, Israel's war on Gaza hasn't just changed the Middle East, it's changed the world | Steve Bloomfield - The Guardian

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T13:20:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Palestinians inspect the damage to an apartment block on Friday after an Israeli strike on Khan Younis. Photograph: Tariq Mohammad/APAImages/Shutterstock
Palestinians inspect the damage to an apartment block on Friday after an Israeli strike on Khan Younis. Photograph: Tariq Mohammad/APAImages/Shutterstock
In three years, Israel’s war on Gaza hasn’t just changed the Middle East, it’s changed the world
A whirlpool of violence has engulfed everywhere from Lebanon to Yemen. Meanwhile, the west has destroyed any political authority it once had
T here was a world before 7 October, 2023, and now there is a world after. For Gaza, for Israel , for the wider Middle East, and for everyone else too. Not all hinge points in history are obvious at the time. Three years on from the day it began, and precisely one year since a supposed ceasefire was agreed, this one is now impossible to ignore.
The before-and-after pictures tell part of the story. An update to Google Maps, released last week , underscores just what three years of relentless Israeli military onslaught has done to Gaza. Satellite images show how entire towns have been reduced to rubble. Mosques and hospitals, visible three years ago, have now disappeared. Meanwhile the once-empty shoreline is now crammed with tents. This is what it looks like when an attempt has been made to destroy a people.
For Israel, too, there has been a before and after. Prime minister Benjamin Netanyahu had cultivated a sense of security, even of impregnability. The ease with which Hamas carried out its terrorist attack, killing 1,200 Israelis, including hundreds of young people at a music festival, destroyed that belief for ever.
Three years on from 7 October, both Israel and Gaza have changed beyond recognition. But the consequences of Hamas’s attack and Israel’s brutal response, which has killed more than 73,000 Palestinians, including 21,000 children, have spread far wider.
Under the pretext of protecting its own borders, Israel has launched wars and military offensives across the region, sending troops into Syria; effectively annexing parts of southern Lebanon, deploying the same tactics there that it used in Gaza; and starting, with the US, an unwinnable war with Iran that has spiralled into a regional conflict affecting all six Gulf states.
The Iran war has sparked a new conflict between the Houthis in Yemen and Saudi Arabia, which this week triggered a mutual defence pact with Pakistan and Turkey, dragging two other countries into the maelstrom. Across the Red Sea, some of the same combatants are involved in the war in Sudan and a fresh conflict inside Ethiopia .
More than a dozen countries across two continents are now entangled in a rising number of wars, with overlapping participants and numerous competing interests.
But there is something else, more insidious, that has changed. The rule of international law, tenuous as it was, has been openly replaced by military might. In 2022, western leaders – building a coalition rightly opposed to Vladimir Putin’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine – made moral appeals based on the violation of international law. In Gaza, they encouraged and supported Israel, even when it was clear that the rules were being broken. That hypocrisy has not gone unnoticed, not just in the global south but among many millions of citizens in the west.
We have watched this happen in real time. Despite the ban on international journalists, anyone with a phone knew what was happening in Gaza from the early days of Israel’s response. Hundreds of Palestinian journalists have reported on the bombing of hospitals and schools, the bombing of cafes and wells, the killings at food distribution points, the deaths of children and babies. War crimes have never before been broadcast in real time, relentlessly, every hour of every day, around the world.
Gaza has also changed how wars are fought. Israel has pioneered the use of AI to select targets, accelerating our descent into a future for which we are unprepared.
A ll of this was allowed to take place in a time when the west could still argue that the “international rules-based order” existed. It may have been destroyed by Donald Trump, but we cannot forget that the dismantling began under Joe Biden. It was Biden who ensured the flow of weapons to Israel continued uninterrupted, Biden who insisted that every United Nations security council resolution calling for a ceasefire was vetoed , and Biden who attacked the international criminal court when it sought to indict Netanyahu for war crimes.
US support for Israel has always been strong and, on the whole, unquestioned. But even Republican presidents have understood they have influence over Israel’s actions and have been prepared to use it.
Ronald Reagan suspended an arms deal in 1981 after Israel annexed the Golan Heights. A year later, his administration stopped supplying cluster weapons to Israel after Menachem Begin’s government used them in populated areas during its invasion of Lebanon. In 2002, the killing of 15 Palestinians in Gaza was enough for Colin Powell, the then secretary of state, to announce a review of Israel’s use of American arms .
Israel was also given a free pass by every other member of the G7, including those run by centrists or centre-left politicians. French president Emmanuel Macron, German chancellor Olaf Scholz, Justin Trudeau in Canada and Australia’s Anthony Albanese all initially gave Israel their backing.
Only António Guterres, the much-maligned UN secretary general, emerges with a modicum of credit. Less than three weeks after 7 October, he called Israel’s response “collective punishment of the Palestinian people” and demanded an immediate ceasefire.
The one ray of hope for the future is that our shared understanding of what has happened over the past three years means that in many countries the political winds have shifted.
Before Andy Burnham even entered Number 10, he apologised for how Labour had handled the early days of the conflict. The UK now has a prime minister who was one of the few western politicians to call for a ceasefire just weeks after 7 October. Ed Miliband’s willingness to accurately describe Israel’s actions in the West Bank as “ethnic cleansing” indicate a change in attitude that could lead to more concerted international action against Israel.
In the US, support for Israel has fallen through the floor. For a Democrat who wants to be president in 2028 there is no credible path to the White House that doesn’t involve support for the Palestinian cause. And yet, we cannot simply close our eyes and hope that a change in America will magically restore the world to how it was on 6 October 2023.
N or should we pretend that the previous era was one we should wish to return to. Did we enter a new world on 7 October, or did we just notice where we were? In the three years before the Hamas attack, the wars in Tigray and Sudan saw many of the same trends play out.
In Tigray, the northernmost part of Ethiopia , a war between the central government and the Tigray People’s Liberation Front killed 600,000 people. There were brutal massacres, forced starvation, widespread use of drones – and absolutely no accountability. In Sudan, too, hospitals have been hit by drones, women have been raped, children have been shot dead. Again, we could see it happen. Again, there were no consequences.
Gaza, though, has undoubtedly accelerated the trend, normalising the immoral. The wars that have begun, that have spiralled out of control and pulled more countries and more actors into their orbit, cannot easily be stopped. There is a momentum to this conflict, a hardening of positions, as each new outrage leads to a response. When there is impunity, when there is no price that those responsible will ever pay, there are terrifyingly few levers to pull. If war crimes go unpunished, they only escalate.
And so a family in Addis Ababa fears the sound of a drone crashing into their home. A village in Lebanon is evacuated, destroyed and the land made uninhabitable. A mundane job at Riyadh airport becomes a daily deadly gamble . A crew of badly paid seafarers pray to their gods as they cross through the strait of Hormuz. The dreams of millions of children shrink from university, fame or fortune, to the brute reality of survival.
And in Gaza, crammed into tents, deprived of enough food and water, two million Palestinians are still living under the brutal rule of Hamas and the daily threat of death from a military force that fears no consequences.
This is our world now. We need to recognise that, if anything is going to change.
Steve Bloomfield is the former international editor of the Observer
