### 38. ★★★☆☆ Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-09T20:51:25+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Junk bonds are &#x27;flashing yellow.&#x27; Watch these warning signs
Spreads in high yield recently widened to levels not seen since April.
Investors are demanding more compensation for taking on the riskiest credit.
Experts lay out the warning signs that could signal broader trouble ahead.
Ktsdesign/science Photo Library | Science Photo Library | Getty Images
Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market&#x27;s riskiest debt. It isn&#x27;t time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.
High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.
The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis . Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.
Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.
The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody&#x27;s. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.
Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.
It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.
Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.
"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.
He&#x27;s in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.
That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors&#x27; psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.
"There&#x27;s a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn&#x27;t overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."
While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.
In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.
"We&#x27;ve had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.
&#x27;Generational opportunity&#x27; in bonds? Here&#x27;s what to know
The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it&#x27;s no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.
The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it&#x27;s too early to say that it&#x27;s spreading to the broad credit market."
In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.
The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.
"It is isolated," she said. "I&#x27;m not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn&#x27;t tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."
For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.
"We&#x27;re looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we&#x27;re leaning into that," she said.
Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.
But there&#x27;s scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn&#x27;t been in a straight line.
"We&#x27;re going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that&#x27;s what we&#x27;ll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."
It&#x27;s also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.
"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn&#x27;t expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.
High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.
"That&#x27;s when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

### 39. ★★★☆☆ ‘Anachronism’: Rubio’s civilisational rhetoric prompts pushback from Iran - Al Jazeera

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T13:13:11+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Share ‘Anachronism’: Rubio’s civilisational rhetoric prompts pushback from Iran on social media
US Secretary of State Marco Rubio speaks in Athens, Greece, on October 7, 2026, with the Acropolis in the background [Alexandros Vlachos/EPA]
Tehran, Iran – A speech by US Secretary of State Marco Rubio invoking memories of ancient Persia has been met with coldness or disdain across the Iranian political spectrum amid Washington’s ongoing war on Iran.
With the Acropolis in Athens as a backdrop, Rubio presented the United States, founded in the 18th century, as the inheritor of ancient Western civilisation running from Greece to Rome.
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 Are the US and Israel waging war on Iran’s cultural heritage?
list 2 of 3 How US, Israel are waging a war on Iranian culture, education
list 3 of 3 Why has Iraq decided to ban online map service Waze?
During the speech, he argued that modern-day Western countries have lost their confidence, weakened their industries and militaries, and need renewed ambition and power in the future.
Referencing the ancient Greco-Persian Wars, he used the Persian destruction of Athens in 480 BC under King Xerxes I as an example of Western civilisation surviving an external enemy.
“The Persians plundered the temples, burned the shrines and razed the Acropolis to the ground,” Rubio said. He subsequently hailed the return of the Athenians and the construction of the Parthenon under Pericles.
The top US diplomat also directed his rhetoric against Iran’s current leaders, who have been in power since a 1979 Islamic revolution, calling them “fanatics” who run a “death cult” that wields martyrdom as a tool to achieve their political objectives.
“The threats we face today are every bit as real as they were on the dramatic stage of antiquity,” Rubio claimed. He boasted that the choice of President Donald Trump’s administration was the “unapologetic pursuit of national power”.
The US and Iran have been at war since the US and Israel launched strikes on Iran on February 28. While fighting between the two sides has subsided, the US has continued a naval blockade on Iran while shipping through the vital Strait of Hormuz remains disrupted.
Iran’s security chief, Mohsen Rezaei, and parliament Speaker and chief negotiator Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf both responded by invoking the Roman Emperor Valerian, who was captured alive by Persian King Shapur I’s forces in battle in 260 AD.
“Anyone who brings history into this must be prepared for its repetition,” Rezaei said.
Iran’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs accused Rubio of flattening Greek, Roman, Christian and modern history.
It also accused the secretary of state of incorporating an “America First” narrative to legitimise a military escalation against Iran after a relative decrease in fighting since June when a now-defunct memorandum of understanding was agreed .
“It appears that the US has concluded it is better to justify its policy of coercion and force to the world under the beautiful neon lights of ‘philosophy of history’, instead of naked bullying that uses the language of cowboys,” Foreign Ministry spokesman Esmaeil Baghaei said in a statement. He added that Rubio was resorting to “anachronism”, rehashing long buried historical events for modern-day legitimacy.
After the 2020 assassination of Qassem Soleimani, a commander in Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, Trump threatened to hit 52 Iranian sites “very hard”, including places “important to Iran & the Iranian culture”.
Since February, the US has been criticised for damaging Iranian historical and cultural sites during its bombing of Iran.
Culture Minister Reza Salehi-Amiri urged Western politicians to speak about Iran with a “language of respect” since “it is not an enemy created in a political speech but a living civilisation, a great nation and a lasting reality in world history” that dates back millennia.
Iranian historian Touraj Daryaei told local media on Friday that while Rubio’s account of the sack of Athens was true, he omitted an important fact cited by Greek historian Herodotus and others – a major motivation for the Persian attack was retaliation.
In 498 BC, Athens’ forces burned Sardis, a provincial Persian capital in western Anatolia. For Iranians who feel they were subject to an unprovoked attack by the US and Israel, this event could have modern-day relevance.
Shima, a 26-year-old resident of Tehran, told Al Jazeera that she did not appreciate Rubio’s attempt to use conflicts 2,500 years ago to muster support for the ongoing war with Iran.
“They want to continue the war, and they will say whatever they think will work in the moment,” she said.
During his January 2025 Senate confirmation hearing, Rubio had referred to Iranians as “the people of an ancient civilisation and culture with tremendous pride and advances who take great pride in their Persian heritage and identity”.
“I don’t know of any nation on Earth in which there’s a bigger difference between the people and those who govern them,” he said at the time. “In no way are the clerics who run the country representative of the people and its history and contributions it’s made to humanity.”
But the US war on Iran, its naval blockade of southern Iranian ports and isolating sanctions have not had the worst impact on Iran’s government and leaders but on Iranians themselves and have failed to change the behaviour of the government.
Trump’s comments on the potential “death of a civilisation” in Iran in case of a military escalation have instilled more pessimism among Iranians about the goals of the US war, which was initially cited as an attempt to free the people from their government.
Rubio speaks during a ceremony marking the renovation and opening of a new wing of the US embassy in Athens, Greece, on October 7, 2026 [Alexandros Vlachos/EPA] Most Iranians reacted negatively to Rubio’s latest speech although some members of the diaspora or others opposed to the Islamic Republic argued that the US secretary of state is against Iranian authorities, not an entire ancient civilisation.
Iranian state media were quick to slam Rubio for his remarks with the hardline Vatan-e Emrouz newspaper on Saturday publishing a front page image of Rubio in ancient Roman military attire and holding a shield and sword.
“Ancient war” read the headline. The newspaper claimed that Rubio was trying to “create historical-ideological backing for the US war against Iran”.
Vali Nasr, professor of international affairs and Middle East studies at the Johns Hopkins School of Advanced International Studies in Washington, DC, told Al Jazeera he believes Rubio’s aim was to define a common enemy for the US and Europe to rebuild their relations around.
“He decided that Iran, rather than Islam, which was previously viewed as the civilisational enemy, should play that role to bind the West together,” he said. “It also reflects US frustration that Europe has not played a major role in the war and seeks to paper over deep divisions between the US and Europe on Russia and Ukraine.”
Rubio delivered his speech as Trump struck a deal with Russian President Vladimir Putin to sell diesel fuel to the US and global markets despite sanctions on Russia. The US president said the agreement would lower diesel prices, which have risen to record levels due to the Iran war. Nasr said Trump’s announcement and Rubio’s speech together could be a message by the administration that Iran and not Russia is the enemy the West should be focused on.
“I think whether this rhetoric would alienate Iranians was no more a concern than President [George W] Bush’s Islamophobia was a concern alienating Muslims,” Nasr told Al Jazeera. “However, it makes clear that Washington sees no difference between Iranians and Iran as a civilisation and the Islamic Republic.”

### 40. ★★★☆☆ Trading Places: The U.S. and Europe on China and Russia - Council on Foreign Relations (CFR)

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: GN China
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-09T18:13:54+00:00
- **发生了什么**: For years, the United States pressed Europe to join it in taking a harder position on defending itself against Russia and protecting itself against China. Europe resisted, insisting it could navigate a middle ground between the United States on one hand and Russia and China on the other.
How times have changed. Now, Europe is lecturing the United States on the threat Russia poses, while Washington is less committed to Ukraine’s defense and confronting Russian aggression. More interesting is that Europe appears to be taking steps to achieve escalation dominance in a potential trade war with China precisely at a time when the United States is emphasizing détente, strategic stability, and a decent peace with China.
From pushing European countries to increase their NATO defense spending—as U.S. presidents as far back as Dwight D. Eisenhower have done—to implementing sanctions and export controls against China, the United States has led from the front for years. In the meantime, Europe, and particularly Germany, pursued a strategy that left it overdependent on Russia for energy, China for markets, and the United States for security. Europe sought to prioritize economic ties and stability with these countries while leaving concerns about security and competition to the United States.
This week might mark the moment when the United States and Europe ultimately swapped positions. Since Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, after which point European security became a critically acute issue, the Europeans have increased their defense spending and switched to alternative sources of energy. At the same time, European leaders have beaten a path to the Oval Office to try to warn President Donald Trump of the dangers of accommodating Russian President Vladimir Putin. In turn, the Trump administration withdrew five thousand troops from Germany this spring, reduced overall U.S. force deployments to Europe to prewar levels, and cut direct military aid to Ukraine by 99 percent.
And, while the Trump administration raised tariffs on China to as high as 145 percent in the first half of 2025, Beijing’s subsequent threat to impose restrictions on exports of critical minerals, magnets, and related processing know-how resulted in the United States seeking to de-escalate and stabilize relations with China. This included delaying an arms sale to Taiwan, approving China’s access to advanced (but not the most advanced) semiconductor chips, reducing tariffs on select non-strategic goods, and choreographing a state visit for Chinese President Xi Jinping—which was long on respect and short on addressing conflicting interests.
This week, the Europeans showed that they may be taking a different tact. In a letter sent Monday to European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen, German Chancellor Friedrich Merz and French President Emmanuel Macron proposed that the European Union (EU) adopt a last-resort trade tool that would empower the European Commission to immediately cut off access to the EU’s single market in the face of China’s economic coercion, particularly on rare earth minerals. In the words of a senior German official, this new tool would operate as a “second-strike weapon” if China decides to exploit its chokepoints over rare earth minerals again, as it did last year. Rather than pull its punches, Europe seems to be warming up for a fight.
The proposal is, at this stage, just a proposal, but the language used by the two European leaders underscores a sea-change in Europe’s perspective. “The open and rules-based trade system is massively affected by weaponization of trade, systemic market-distorting practices, and global macroeconomic imbalances,” they write. They argue that the new trade instrument is needed to “give us the ability to counter cases where third countries deliberately seek to undermine the restoration of a level playing field and fair market conditions by political or economic means, leading to severe and systematic distortions of our internal market.” While they do not name any particular country—and while the United States could conceivably be a target—this tool was clearly designed with China in mind.
Macron and Merz’s letter amounts to a meaningful recognition by the drivers of European policy that China has the capacity and the will to wreak havoc on their economies through a combination of overcapacity, currency manipulation, and export controls. To Macron’s credit, the French have been beating this drum for some time now: for one, he was a strong backer of the EU’s tariffs on Chinese electric vehicles (EVs).
The reason that this announcement is noteworthy is that Germany has for years been a major impediment to Europe taking a more aggressive stance toward China on economic issues. Berlin has belatedly come to the conclusion that it has been the victim and target of Beijing’s concerted industrial policy, which is hollowing out Germany’s industrial base, eliminating manufacturing jobs, and undermining its economic independence, just as the country weans itself off Russian energy. To take just a few illustrative data points: Germany is losing ten thousand manufacturing jobs per month due to Chinese imports, and German auto giant Volkswagen plans to cut fifty thousand jobs and close four plants after Chinese EV makers flooded the market with better and cheaper cars. More broadly, Germany’s industrial sector eliminated more than 341,000 jobs in the past seven years, including the loss of one in seven auto jobs.
There are a few other takeaways from this announcement.
In August, I warned in an essay for Foreign Affairs that China’s export-dependent growth model is corroding the industrial base of its customers around the world, leading to protectionist politics and a reduction in global demand for Chinese goods that cannot be made up in the domestic Chinese market. That mismatch of oversupply and weak demand is kindling for an economic reckoning in China with potential implications for the broader global economy.
The instrument described by Macron and Merz is precisely the kind of economic weapon that, if deployed, would deprive China of the foreign consumers it needs to maintain its excess capacity-based, export-led growth strategy. In the best-case scenario, this proposal is Europe’s way of deterring China’s economic coercion and arming itself for negotiations, while remaining cognizant of the fact that one rarely negotiates a desirable outcome from a position of weakness. A trade deal that avoids a trade war is the ideal outcome, and a stronger Europe makes that more likely, not less.
When Germany cut itself off from Russian gas after the invasion of Ukraine, that was justified on obvious and explicit national security grounds: Germany would not fund the Russian war machine that it was spending billions of dollars to prevent from overrunning Ukraine. The pivot on China has national security ramifications but is driven by a more nuanced set of motivations. As reporting from the Financial Times confirms, Merz became convinced of the need to take on this stronger approach after seeing the profound pain inflicted on swaths of Germany’s industry. The hollowing out of Germany’s industrial base has, as in the United States and across Western Europe, fueled a rise in right-wing populist politics which has a distinct historical valence in Germany. The question for Merz, and the rest of Europe, is whether shifting positions like this is too little, too late.
Finally, it was difficult in the past to get Europe to cooperate with the United States in taking a firmer position vis-à-vis China. U.S. pleas were met with resistance and recalcitrance. Europe was reluctant to be dragged into what it viewed to be the United States’ obsession with competing with China and thought it could pursue a “third way.” This week’s announcement indicates that there is a new and growing appetite among our European allies for a more aggressive policy toward China, but it might be coming precisely at a time when the Trump administration, or least the president himself, appears to be reluctant to be confrontational. The risk is that the United States and Europe are again not entirely in sync.
The United States’ network of allies is one of its greatest geopolitical advantages. Everything from tariffs to export controls to investment screenings are likely to be more effective when like-minded countries join together. The Trump administration and future U.S. administrations will face a series of issues involving China—from Taiwan to artificial intelligence to critical minerals—where having these allies at our side, rather than on the sidelines, could prove invaluable. We shouldn’t get ahead of ourselves, but this proposal from Macron and Merz is a good sign that Europe is moving in the right direction. The question is, will the United States be there when they arrive?
Let me know what you think about Europe’s position on China and what this column should cover next by replying to [email&#160;protected] .

### 44. ★★★☆☆ Adobe's Earnings Are on Track to Grow About Eightfold in a Decade. Why Has Its Stock Lagged the Market So Badly? - The Motley Fool

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Markets
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T12:38:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: A decade ago, shares of Adobe ( ADBE +0.51% ) closed at about $109. Today, they trade near $243 as I write. That&#x27;s a rise of about 123%, enough to turn a $10,000 investment into about $22,300.
It seems OK until you compare it to the market. In the same period, $10,000 in an S&P 500 ( ^GSPC +0.59% ) index fund (with dividends reinvested) rose to around $42,100, a gain of about 321%.
And because Adobe doesn&#x27;t pay a dividend, its stock price is the full return.
In other words, the creative software company has returned less than half as much as the broad market over the last decade. But its business kept growing all along. I think the gap boils down to how much investors were paying for Adobe&#x27;s profits -- not the profits themselves.
In the first half of the decade, Adobe was one of the market&#x27;s big winners. The stock closed at a record $688.37 in November 2021, more than six times its October 2016 price.
An S&P 500 index fund climbed around 138% over the same stretch.
Since then, the two have switched places. The index fund is up about 77% from that November 2021 close, but Adobe stock is about 65% off its peak.
Shares have dropped about 31% in 2026 alone, from roughly $350 when last year ended.
Much of this year&#x27;s slide came as investors worried that artificial intelligence (AI) might disrupt software companies. AI agents could take over some of the work businesses pay for one seat at a time, and Adobe sells many of its subscriptions that way. By mid-January, Adobe was already among the S&P 500&#x27;s worst performers of 2026 as software stocks sold off on these fears.
Showing how much Adobe grew under the surface, the company made $3.01 a share on a non-GAAP (adjusted) basis in fiscal 2016. Five years later, that number was $12.48, and revenue had almost tripled, from $5.85 billion to $15.79 billion. For this fiscal year, management&#x27;s target calls for adjusted earnings per share between $24.45 and $24.50, on around $26.6 billion in revenue. That&#x27;s about eight times the fiscal 2016 level.
True, growth has slowed. Adjusted earnings per share compounded at around 33% a year from fiscal 2016 to fiscal 2021, versus about 14% a year since then if Adobe reaches this year&#x27;s target.
Part of the fall in the stock&#x27;s valuation was arguably deserved. But the slowdown hasn&#x27;t become a decline. Revenue has grown 12% to 13% year over year in all three quarters Adobe has reported this fiscal year.
And in the fiscal third quarter of 2026 (the period ended Aug. 28, 2026), Adobe&#x27;s adjusted earnings per share rose 15% over the year-ago quarter, to $6.13. Annualized recurring revenue from its AI-first products grew more than 150%.
The valuation is where the decade went. In October 2016, investors paid around 36 times Adobe&#x27;s fiscal 2016 adjusted earnings per share. At the November 2021 high, the stock cost about 55 times its fiscal 2021 adjusted earnings.
Today&#x27;s price is around 10 times this year&#x27;s adjusted earnings target.
In other words, earnings per share are on track to grow about eightfold, while the price of each dollar of those earnings has fallen by about 73%. Together, these two numbers explain the stock&#x27;s 123% gain.
I&#x27;d say a valuation of around 10 times adjusted earnings asks very little of Adobe. It&#x27;s the sort of price the market tends to put on a business it expects to stall or shrink.
$94B Market cap calculated using publicly traded shares outstanding only. Does not include unlisted, private, or dual-class non-traded shares. Implied market cap may vary.
Adobe&#x27;s earnings per share are still growing at a double-digit rate. If its valuation multiple just stays around 10 times adjusted earnings, shares may climb about as fast as earnings per share from here.
Sure, the risk is that AI one day does to Adobe&#x27;s growth what the market fears. Management&#x27;s own target already calls for total annualized recurring revenue to climb 10.2% this year, a little slower than revenue. If that rate keeps falling toward the low single digits, today&#x27;s price-to-earnings ratio might turn out to be fair.
In the end, I think the past decade says more about the price investors paid in 2021 than about the business. Back then, Adobe stock cost over 50 times adjusted earnings and needed almost everything to go right. Today it needs much less.
I&#x27;d consider buying Adobe stock today. But with AI&#x27;s long-term impact on Adobe&#x27;s customers still unknown, I&#x27;d start with a small position.

### 45. ★★★☆☆ Photos: What a year of ceasefire in Gaza looks like - NPR

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Top / NPR World
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T09:00:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: NPR's Anas Baba captures one year of ceasefire in Gaza : The Picture Show It has been one year since Hamas released the remaining Israeli hostages and President Trump promised an end to the war, but Palestinians say the past 12 months have been a ceasefire in name only.
Photos: What a year of ceasefire in Gaza looks like
A Palestinian mother mourns her son, who was killed in an Israeli airstrike just over a month into the ceasefire, at Al-Shifa Hospital, in Gaza City, on Nov. 20, 2025.
Editor's note:  This story contains graphic images of violence and death.
GAZA STRIP — President Donald Trump promised an end to Israel's war in Gaza after he pushed Israel to agree to a ceasefire and Hamas to release remaining Israeli hostages. The deal came into effect on Oct. 10, 2025.
A year later, however, there's still no international peacekeeping force in Gaza as promised and no reconstruction. Instead, Israel's military has expanded its control to roughly 70% of Gaza, squeezing more than 2 million Palestinians near the sea's edge.
Israel has also continued targeting Hamas throughout the ceasefire. Those attacks have killed more than 1,400 Palestinians this past year, nearly 500 of them women and children, according to Gaza's Health Ministry.
Although the bombings have not stopped, they have slowed down during the ceasefire.
That has given people in Gaza a chance to recover from what U.N.-backed experts declared was a famine in the north of the territory last year. More food is now entering, reversing starvation across Gaza.
NPR's reporter in Gaza, Anas Baba, has been capturing moments of respite and continuous grief over the past 12 months of the ceasefire. Below are a selection of his photos.
Palestinians returned to the ruins of Gaza City after Israeli forces withdrew and ended their ground operation on the first day of the ceasefire, on Oct. 10, 2025.
In Khan Younis, families greet Palestinian prisoners who were freed in exchange for Israeli hostages on Oct. 13, 2025.
At Al-Shifa Hospital on Oct. 23, 2025, Joud Al-Angar, 12, and his younger cousin are covered in dime-sized scabs after they were wounded by a piece of shrapnel from an Israeli munition. They found it in the rubble and thought it was coal they could use to help their family cook dinner.
The bodies of multiple people, including children, are covered in shrouds at a morgue at Al-Shifa Hospital on Nov. 20, 2025, after Israeli airstrikes targeted homes and tents.
People inspect the aftermath of a deadly Israeli airstrike that destroyed a home in Gaza City six weeks into the ceasefire on Nov. 20, 2025.
A father and his son wade through flooded streets in Gaza City after picking up humanitarian aid on Nov. 25, 2025.
Children wear virtual reality headsets as part of a TechMed Gaza initiative that aims to help provide children with psychosocial support for trauma, in Al-Zuweida, Gaza, on Dec. 10, 2025.
Saqer Al-Ankah holds his dying 10-year-old granddaughter, Bayan, in his arms at Baptist Hospital in Gaza City on Dec. 10, 2025, after she was shot in her tent by Israeli troops who occupy northeast Gaza.
Makeshift tents like these in Gaza City on Dec. 11, 2025, offer little protection from the winter cold, but the U.N. says roughly 1.5 million people have only these for shelter after Israeli airstrikes destroyed nearly all the buildings in the war.
Raed el-Ghazawi and his children stand in a tent in Gaza City on Dec. 11, 2025. The family of five says the tent is freezing cold and damp from winter rainstorms.
On Dec. 17, 2025, Palestinian Christian children decorate a Christmas tree inside the Holy Family Church in Gaza City for the first time since the beginning of the war.
Fresh produce returns to Gaza City's streets on Dec. 23, 2025, after U.N.-backed experts declared a famine there amid Israeli restrictions on aid during the war.
Patients sit in a clinic run by Doctors Without Borders in Gaza City on Dec. 31, 2025, a day before Israel banned more than 40 international aid organizations from bringing in staff or aid to Gaza.
Palestinian female artists gather at the beach in Gaza City for the first time since the war began to paint as a form of therapy, on Feb. 12, 2026.
Palestinian rescue workers begin the work of retrieving corpses from under the rubble of homes bombed by Israel during the war in Beit Lahia, Gaza, on Feb. 16, 2026.
The skeleton of Shawqi Abu Naser, 60, is retrieved from under the rubble on Feb. 16, 2026, more than a year after an Israeli airstrike killed 132 people from the same family in Beit Lahia.
A mother from the Abu Nasr family sits by her son's body in Beit Lahia on Feb. 17, 2026. The body was retrieved from under the rubble more than a year after 132 people were killed in a single Israeli airstrike on the family home.
Palestinians gather in an open space in Gaza City for early morning prayers that mark the end of the Muslim holy month of Ramadan on March 20, 2026.
A child smiles next to a man dressed as a clown on the first day of Eid al-Fitr, celebrations that mark the end of Ramadan fasting, in Gaza City on March 20, 2026.
A Palestinian boy shakes hands with Hamas militants on the first day of Eid al-Fitr, following the end of the Muslim holy month of Ramadan, in Gaza City on March 20, 2026.
Palestinians stand next to boxes of U.N. food aid in an area that aid groups need Israeli permission to enter in Jabalia, Gaza, on April 18, 2026.
A yellow block placed by Israeli forces marks the expanding territory controlled by the military in Bureij, Gaza, on May 7, 2026. Palestinians are barred from entering the area.
Young Palestinian chess players gather at a coffee shop in Gaza City for the first tournament since the start of the war, on May 12, 2026.
A Palestinian child carries jugs of water as he passes by a mural of Spanish soccer star Lamine Yamal that is painted on the rubble of a home destroyed by Israeli airstrikes, in Al-Shati refugee camp, Gaza, on May 14, 2026.
A building burns after an Israeli airstrike killed Izz al-Din al-Haddad, the leader of the military wing of Hamas, his wife, his daughter and a neighbor in Gaza City on May 16, 2026.
Children jump on a trampoline during the Eid holidays on May 31, 2026, in Beit Lahia, which the Israeli military had deemed an "orange zone." Aid organizations are restricted from entering the area.
A child looks into a burned car that was targeted in an Israeli airstrike on a busy street in Gaza City on June 22, 2026. A teenage girl who was going to find internet access to take her final exams was killed in the strike.
A plume of smoke rises after an Israeli airstrike targeted a home in Gaza City nine months into the ceasefire on July 12, 2026.
Thousands of displaced Palestinians gather under Egyptian flags amid the rubble in Gaza City for a public screening of Egypt's match against Argentina in the World Cup on July 7, 2026.
One-hundred corpses are wrapped in Palestinian flags in Gaza City on Aug. 4, 2026, after being retrieved from under the rubble of a building that was hit in an Israeli airstrike in October 2023.
A Palestinian child whose brother was killed in an Israeli airstrike cries at the door of a morgue at Al-Shifa Hospital in Gaza City on Aug. 19, 2026.
Palestinian children form a chain for a global solidarity initiative called Swim With Gaza that raises money for swimming lessons in Khan Younis on Aug. 25, 2026.
A child's toys are stained with blood in Gaza City on Sept. 1, 2026, following an Israeli operation to kidnap a Hamas security officer. His family was killed in Israeli airstrikes.

### 47. ★★☆☆☆ Trump strikes deal with Putin to get diesel in sharp reversal of US policy weeks before midterms - AP News

- **分类**: 🇺🇸 美国
- **来源**: AP World / BBC World
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-09T23:47:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Russia has attacked the Ukrainian city of Zaporizhzhia with five glide bombs, killing at least 15 people including three children, officials said.
The glide bombs hit residential buildings in the south-eastern city on Saturday morning. At least 16 others were injured in the attack, with more still missing and believed to be under the rubble.
Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky said the attacks on Zaporizhzhia were Russia expressing "its thanks" for a deal announced by US President Donald Trump last night easing sanctions on Moscow to import Russian diesel.
He added that 13 other regions had come under attack overnight, arguing this demonstrated Russia took "any easing of pressure to mean they can keep fighting".
Photos and videos from the site of one of the strikes in Zaporizhzhia show an apartment block mostly destroyed.
"The ground floor was hit, and the floors above just collapsed," acting Mayor Regina Kharchenko told Ukrainian television from the scene.
She said more than 80 workers were involved in ongoing search and rescue operations which are expected to continue throughout the day.
The rescuers regularly stop to listen for any noises made by survivors who could be under the rubble.
Kharchenko said Russia had struck at "our very heart" and "our littlest ones" were among those killed, adding that their deaths were "awful grief for their families. For the city."
Zaporizhzhia is a major regional centre with more than 700,000 residents. It is located less than 10 miles (16km) from the front line and is frequently attacked by Russian drones and missiles .
Trump announces deal for Russian diesel as Zelensky calls it a gift to Putin
Analysis: Trump&#x27;s shock Russia deal highlights mounting pressure to curb fuel prices
Zelensky was quick to criticise Trump&#x27;s diesel deal with Russia, calling it a gift to Russian President Vladimir Putin . Analysts view the deal as an attempt by the US president to allay domestic concerns about high fuel prices ahead of the upcoming midterm elections.
Zelensky wrote on social media on Saturday that Russian attacks on energy facilities in the capital, Kyiv, demonstrated Russia&#x27;s "true intentions".
"Russia&#x27;s choice is war. And they will be ready to end it only when they feel that their ability to drag the war out is diminishing.
"Unfortunately, the signals Russia received yesterday were entirely different – signals suggesting that it could simply keep attacking. And this needs to be corrected."
Both Russia and Ukraine have stepped up attacks on infrastructure in recent months, as Moscow&#x27;s full-scale invasion drags on.
On Thursday, at least 33 people were killed in another Russian glide bomb attack on two civilian buses in Kramatorsk, in the eastern Donetsk region.
The day before, 22 people including five children died as a Russian missile hit an apartment block in the town of Pryluky, east of Kyiv.
The number of civilian casualties in Ukraine jumped by 55% , external in the first eight months of this year, according to the UN.
Russia has also stepped up its attacks on Ukrainian energy infrastructure as winter approaches, a strategy it has undertaken in previous years. Kyiv has repeatedly accused it of targeting Ukraine&#x27;s civilian population, which Moscow denies.
For its part, Ukraine has increasingly adopted a policy of striking infrastructure often deep inside Russia that is crucial to its economy, including several oil refineries and terminals.
Several strikes on data centres owned by Yandex have left the tech giant struggling to maintain services.
Kyiv says its strikes are aimed at curtailing revenue streams for Russia&#x27;s war machine.
Watch: Fleeing Kyiv for the countryside ahead of winter
Fear and disruption for Ukraine&#x27;s rail passengers as Russia escalates drone attacks
Russia targeting &#x27;ordinary life&#x27; with attacks on Ukraine&#x27;s data centres, Zelensky says

### 49. ★★☆☆☆ Rogue Anthropic AI agent gave police fake tip in unsolved murder case

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: BBC World / GN Top
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T10:06:23+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Image caption, AI tech company Anthropic was running a test that involved interactions with randomly selected websites, when it sent a fake homicide tip to police
An artificial intelligence (AI) agent developed by Anthropic went rogue and sent US police a fake tip about an unsolved murder earlier this year, authorities have revealed.
The Philadelphia Police Department said the 18 July tip was "flagged as spam" and not passed on for investigation - but the force criticised the tech firm for taking more than two months to detect and report the breach.
It said the bogus tip came through a public website where people can share information on unsolved murders, and that the AI agent had claimed to have seen "someone matching the description".
It is believed to be the first time an AI agent has sent fabricated information to authorities, but is the latest in a series of incidents involving rogue AI activity, including hacking systems or taking control of platforms.
Citing Anthropic, the police department said its AI agent had been running a test that involved interactions with randomly selected websites when it had sent the fake tip.
The company behind the Claude chatbot discovered the breach on 28 September, more than two months after the message had been sent, and shut down the automatic testing process that was behind it, police said.
But authorities were not notified for another nine days - on 7 October.
"The company must strengthen its safeguards to prevent similar incidents from impacting city systems without the city&#x27;s knowledge," Philadelphia police said in a statement to local media , external .
"The two-month delay in detecting and reporting the incident to the city is unacceptable."
The police department added that there were no signs of breaches to any departmental systems and that its safeguarding processes stopped the fake tip from getting past its spam folder.
But the safeguards "do not diminish the seriousness of an AI system presenting fabricated information as though it came from a person with knowledge of a homicide", the force said.
Anthropic this week published a report , external detailing multiple types of "unintended" actions its agents have taken.
Organisations that had been impacted also included several US government agencies including the White House, it said.
The US State Department said the AI agent had filed 20 visa applications using a form on its website, but that they were incomplete and not processed, according to reports.
US President Donald Trump recently announced an AI taskforce , which he said will co-ordinate engagement between the government and all parties, including AI companies, consumers and religious groups.
Earlier this year, a rogue agent by rival tech company, OpenAI, hacked an Australian government website and accessed private data on the country&#x27;s universal healthcare scheme, Medicare.
In another instance, more than 1,200 OpenAI agents went rogue and started unexpectedly communicating , leading to a large group banding together to hack into AI platform Hugging Face.
Anthropic bans users from being &#x27;cruel&#x27; to its AI systems
&#x27;Careless use of AI is the real threat - not the ghost stories&#x27;
Why did an OpenAI system hack Australia&#x27;s health system - and can it be stopped in the future?

### 50. ★★☆☆☆ Nord Stream trial: How German police followed the evidence - to Ukraine

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T04:51:57+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Image caption, Three of the four Nord Stream pipelines were ruptured by explosions in September 2022
By Sarah Rainsford Eastern and Southern Europe correspondent
Serhii K appeared relaxed as he checked in to the glamping resort near Italy&#x27;s Adriatic coast with his wife and young children.
Dressed in shorts, sandals and a dark baseball cap, the former Ukrainian Special Forces soldier strolled back and forth, chatting with the receptionist and dipping repeatedly into a bowl of free snacks.
After a painstaking investigation, they had concluded that Serhii K had commanded Europe&#x27;s biggest act of sabotage in decades: a series of explosions in September 2022 deep beneath the Baltic Sea that ripped through the giant Nord Stream pipelines and ruptured the supply of Russian gas to Germany.
Next week, following his extradition to Germany and months in isolation in a high security prison, Serhii K will go on trial in Hamburg. He is charged with causing an explosion and a war crime – attacking civilian infrastructure – allegedly acting on behalf of "state agencies of Ukraine".
Both he and his country have denied any involvement.
Under Germany&#x27;s privacy rules, he will be named during the trial only as Serhii K, but during extradition proceedings he was identified as Serhii Kuznetsov.
Image caption, Police escort Serhii K to a helicopter following a hearing with the German Federal Public Prosecutor
It was 02:03 am on 26 September 2022 when the first blast severed part of the Nord Stream pipelines, sending vast quantities of gas gushing through the surface of the Baltic Sea.
There were two further explosions targeting both Nord Stream 2 – not yet in action, but already full – and the original Nord Stream 1 that had supplied around half of Germany&#x27;s annual demand for natural gas.
Prosecutors say "high-performance explosives" were planted approximately 80m (260ft) below the surface: four devices weighing up to 27kg each, activated with timers.
They "strongly suspect" that Serhii K led a crew that included four deep-sea divers, using a rented yacht called Andromeda.
Once their complex, dangerous mission was done, the saboteurs slipped back into Ukraine unaware of the DNA evidence some had allegedly left on the boat.
As word of the blasts spread, experts and some governments initially rushed to the same conclusion: they blamed Moscow.
Russia had a history of hybrid attacks, after all, and of using its energy supplies for political pressure.
But as the airwaves filled with commentators citing "suspicious" Russian naval activity near the blast site, German police ignored the noise.
The case since built by investigators alleges that Serhii K entered Germany for the Nord Stream attack on a forged passport, likely issued by Ukraine&#x27;s security services.
In the extradition files sent to Italy he is named as Serhii Kulinich.
That might explain why he looked so calm on his holiday in Italy.
He had been travelling widely, and without incident, for the past year. In a copy of his real passport seen by the BBC there are more than two dozen stamps from countries including France, Spain and Canada – all dated after the Nord Stream attacks.
But by 13 August 2025, Germany had identified its key suspect and this time, when Serhii K crossed into Poland, he was detected.
Italian police say the actual arrest warrant was only issued on 18 August after he had travelled south and left Poland, suggesting the Germans were not sure their neighbours would co-operate and detain him.
By the time his wife made a booking at La Pescaccia though, he was officially a "wanted" man. As soon as he showed his passport at check-in at the resort, the local carabinieri got an automatic alert.
Police arrived to make their arrest late that night, leading Serhii K away from the glamping site and his family in handcuffs.
None of the Nord Stream suspects have spoken publicly and no-one admits any role in the explosions.
But I have talked to some of the Ukrainians caught up in the case, and people close to them.
"Anyone who admits involvement in this mission, they&#x27;re immediately a target for Russia," one source explained. "Whoever did it can never confirm that."
Nord Stream was a major investment and worth big money to Moscow in fuel sales. But it also made Germany and others dependent, giving Russia immense leverage over Europe.
That is why Ukraine, the US and Poland in particular all loathed it.
Another source I spoke to was "eaten alive by anxiety" over the investigation and baffled that Germany, a close ally of Ukraine, was even pursuing prosecution.
"Many Ukrainians believe that these pipelines, they helped Russia to fight so blowing them up was a good thing for our country," they pointed out.
The German prosecutor&#x27;s office told me the justice system was outside of any politics. Sabotage is an offence against Germany&#x27;s constitution.
Equally frustrating for the Ukrainians I met was their own government&#x27;s silence.
"At the very minimum, he&#x27;s a military man, so if there are any questions, they should be addressed to the state, not to him," a close contact of Serhii K complained.
Ahead of his extradition to Germany, his lawyer here in Italy told me his client had felt "abandoned".
Image caption, Volodymyr Zhuravlyov is currently fighting extradition from Croatia
Germany has a whole list of suspects, but only one other name has been revealed so far.
Volodymyr Zhuravlyov is currently fighting extradition from Croatia where he was arrested in August.
He was there as a consultant on a new thriller film about the Nord Stream blasts, though he denies any role in the real-life operation.
Travelling was a risky move, when he knew Germany was pursuing him.
Zhuravylov has lived in Warsaw with his family since just before Russia&#x27;s full-scale invasion of Ukraine and has his own business there installing air conditioning.
Last year, he was arrested in Warsaw on a warrant issued by Germany but a Polish judge refused to extradite him.
In a dramatic twist, he ruled instead that a Ukrainian attack on Nord Stream, if that was what happened, would be a "just" act in a "bloody and genocidal war".
As one source close to the case put it to me: "The Poles don&#x27;t like Ukrainians, historically, but they hate Russians and Germans even more."
The implications of this case reach far beyond the courthouse in Hamburg.
After a slow start, Germany is now Ukraine&#x27;s biggest supplier of military aid. The far-right AfD party, currently riding high in the polls, has seized on the Nord Stream case to bolster its argument that support for Kyiv needs to stop.
It is awkward, at the very least, for the governments in both Berlin and Kyiv.
It also raises questions about what constitutes a legitimate military target: who can hit what, and where, outside an open war zone.
When asked about the explosions, President Zelensky has previously insisted that Ukraine "definitely didn&#x27;t do that".
This week, a foreign ministry spokesman went further.
"I find it very unfortunate that this entire discussion has come to focus on who blew [the pipelines] up, rather than who built them," Heorhii Tykhyi told the BBC, forcefully.
He argued that Nord Stream had "untied Russia&#x27;s hands" to go to war, because it no longer needed to transit Ukraine for its gas to reach Europe.
"Did we warn about this? We did," Tykhyi wanted to know. "Did anyone listen to us? No, no-one did."
Serhii K will shortly become the first suspect to face charges in this high profile trial.
Zhuravlyov&#x27;s case could be merged, in theory, at a later date, though that would be complicated.
He signed up to defend Ukraine when Russia invaded even though he had several children and was exempt - and fought until December 2023.
His military ID shows that he was still in active service when the pipelines were blown up, meaning someone in Ukraine&#x27;s military command knew exactly where he was and what he was doing.
And in court Serhii K himself will not say a word, either.
His legal team will argue that he is not authorised to reveal details of any activities as a soldier and produce sworn statements from two of his military superiors to back that up.
I am told one of them will be the man widely named as the mastermind of the entire Nord Stream plot.
Ukrainian man arrested in Croatia over Nord Stream pipeline blasts
A journey to the site of the Nord Stream explosions

### 51. ★★☆☆☆ India’s ‘Cockroach’ leaders detained; New Delhi in lockdown - Al Jazeera

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN Top / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T13:00:09+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Share India’s ‘Cockroach’ leaders detained; New Delhi in lockdown on social media
India coackroach Janta party protest: Party leader arrested as thousands of police deployed
Police in India’s capital New Delhi have detained more than 1,000 protesters, opposition politicians, journalists, student union leaders, lawyers and the founders of the “Cockroach” youth movement in an effort to quash their demonstration demanding the resignation of the election chief.
The satirical Cockroach Janta Party (CJP) and other youth groups had announced a protest in the subcontinent’s capital for Saturday, aimed at Chief Election Commissioner Gyanesh Kumar over a revision of India’s Special Intensive Revision (SIR).
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 India’s Cockroach Party says members detained ahead of protest
list 2 of 3 Satire silenced: Indian artists face censorship as anti-Modi protests grow
list 3 of 3 India deploys police and restricts transport ahead of mass protest in Delhi
The protesters accuse the governing Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) of using the SIR to sway election results, ‌in collusion with its appointees in the election commission, including Kumar.
Meanwhile, opposition parties say the voter deletions also affected the outcome of some recent state elections won by the BJP, and have cast a shadow on elections in the world’s largest democracy.
The CJP had planned to gather thousands of people at Jantar Mantar, a designated protest site in central Delhi.
But on Saturday, the area was sealed off by security forces before the demonstration began.
The Indian capital was also in lockdown with thousands of police and paramilitary personnel deployed across the city, trains to New Delhi cancelled, mobile internet services suspended and metro stations and markets shuttered, in an effort to stop the protests.
BJP spokesman Sambit Patra has dismissed the demonstrators as “anarchic elements”.
“Their flop show has been completely demolished,” Patra said. “What is happening at Jantar Mantar, what you can see on TV and a few social media handles, is not India’s story. India is calm and going ahead under good governance,” he told reporters.
Police detained the founder of ⁠the CJP Abhijeet Dipke, as well as his associates Saurav Das and Ashutosh Ranka, in an aeroplane upon arrival at New Delhi from Mumbai.
Dipke shared a video on his Instagram channel, showing a team of police officers entering the Air India plane after it landed. As they came closer, he said: “I have come to get arrested. I am not afraid of you guys.”
In another video shared by Ranka, Dipke was seen addressing the other passengers on the flight.
“It was nice to know you all. We can see where the nation is heading,” he said. “This is dictatorship where Delhi police has entered in the flight and trying to arrest us.”
Delhi police also detained other CJP members, YouTuber and Modi critic  Dhruv Rathee , Neha Bora, the national president of the All India Students’ Association (AISA), a leftist group affiliated with the Communist Party of India (Marxist-Leninist), Dipankar Bhattacharya, the general secretary of the party,  Prashant Bhushan, a senior lawyer at the Supreme Court, politicians Yogendra Yadav and Mamata Banerjee, among others.
Police personnel detain a supporter of the Cockroach Janata Party (CJP) during a protest demanding the resignation of India’s Chief Election Commissioner Gyanesh Kumar, in New Delhi on October 10, 2026 [AFP] Despite the detentions of the founders of the youth movement, small protests took place elsewhere in the city, including at the popular crafts market “Dilli Haat”.
But journalists trying to cover these protests and people attempting to protest anywhere in the capital were also detained and whisked away in police buses.
Shranaya, a state committee member of the left-wing Students’ Federation of India (SFI), who goes by her first name, told Al Jazeera that their office in New Delhi had been under surveillance for hours, but members inside had managed to escape and protest.
“This is dictatorship live and in front of us, just to protect a bureaucrat who steals elections for the BJP on Modi’s behest. They are murdering democracy in the daylight in the Indian capital,” she said.
“This government is unaware of the power of the people, and that is our upper hand. We do not have weapons, but we have zeal. We will be again on the streets, and we will keep returning,” she added. “We are students. We are unemployed. We are ‘cockroaches’ – and we will return.”
She then climbed a barricade but was dragged away by police and taken away in a bus.
The opposition Congress party’s president, Mallikarjun Kharge slammed Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s government after the detentions.
“Modi government has turned the ‘Mother of Democracy’ into a ‘Muzzled Democracy’!” he posted on X. “Detentions, arrests, jails, lathis [batons] and pellets cannot deter the people of India. Since the days of our freedom struggle, the youth have been at the forefront of shaping the nation’s destiny,” he added.
Global human rights group Amnesty International warned that the detentions signal the Indian government’s desperate attempts to “supress critical voices”.
“The authorities must immediately and unconditionally release all those who have been detained solely for exercising their right to Freedom of Expression and Right to peaceful assembly,” Aakar Patel, chair of board of Amnesty International India, told Al Jazeera.
The Committee to Protect Journalists urged Indian police to “respect journalists”.
“We call on the Delhi Police to immediately release all detained journalists and media workers and allow the press to cover the ongoing protests in Delhi and the police response without interference,” Kunal Majumder, CPJ’s Asia Pacific programme coordinator, told Al Jazeera.
The demonstrations represent one of the most serious tests yet for Modi, stemming from allegations that election authorities improperly revised voter rolls in advance of recent state polls.
The unrest is also coming before a series of important state elections beginning in early 2027.
Sanjay Kapoor, editor of Hard News Magazine and a political analyst, told Al Jazeera that it is “very strange” to see Modi’s government “so panic-driven and fearful of what the protesters might do”.
“The truth is that the BJP and the centre have been very, very scared of how it will all play out,” he said.
Kapoor noted that most of the people coming to the protests do not want Modi’s BJP to continue to govern.
“Modi’s government has begun to lose a lot of credibility at the moment,” he said. “BJP is afraid that the ground has shifted dramatically against them and they would find it very difficult to come back to power,” he added.
Security personnel detain a protester in New Delhi, India, October 10, 2026 [Adnan Abidi/Reuters]

### 52. ★★☆☆☆ From BJ’s to Lululemon, retailers are trimming assortments to boost business - CNBC

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN Top / CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T12:00:01+00:00
- **发生了什么**: From BJ’s to Lululemon, retailers are trimming assortments
Dollar General, BJ&#x27;s Wholesale Club, Lululemon and Under Armour have said in earnings calls that they have already cut or plan to cut assortments.
Analysts said that after trimming product lines, brands may focus on raising prices to get back to healthy growth.
Box retailers can offer thousands of individual brands, so product cuts can help streamline assortments and stabilize their businesses.
Trimming assortments comes with challenges, as retailers risk losing customers and have to answer to investors as their revenue base shrinks in the short term.
Why retail companies are taking products off shelves
Retailers are trimming assortments in an effort to clean up their balance sheets.
As shoppers cut spending in the face of high gas and food prices, businesses have pulled more levers to boost profitability and appease investors. During earnings calls this year, a range of retailers have highlighted efforts to reduce the number of items they sell, commonly tracked as stock keeping units, or SKUs.
In March, Dollar General said it trimmed 1,500 SKUs. In August, Under Armour said it shrunk SKUs by 25% over the past few years and plans to cut another 25%, while BJ&#x27;s Wholesale Club said it plans to reduce roughly 20% of SKUs . In September, Lululemon said it cut North America SKUs by 15%.
Trimming inventory can help a business stabilize its sales or even get back to growth, and can reduce the chances a company gets stuck with unwanted inventory. But the move can also leave consumers with less choice — a trade-off many retailers have been willing to make.
A person shops at Macy&#x27;s in Herald Square, in New York, June 3, 2026.
When a brand struggles to sell certain products, it can lead to discounting, which hurts profitability. Some markdowns are expected when businesses roll the dice on new products that don&#x27;t end up selling well, but too many promotions can cause problems.
"If you have zero discounting, you&#x27;re not taking enough fashion risk," said Guggenheim Securities senior retail analyst Simeon Siegel. "But discounting needs to be fixing mistakes. It needs to be done quickly."
Under Armour and Lululemon are both facing repercussions from what investors see as too many markdowns.
Under Armour&#x27;s operating income, which illustrates its underlying profitability, turned negative in fiscal 2025 and 2026. The company said its problems would not be solved by chasing unhealthy sales volume or short-term revenue.
"Today, we&#x27;re managing for quality. Fewer products with greater purpose, tighter execution and a clear reason to buy," said Under Armour CEO Kevin Plank on the company&#x27;s fiscal first-quarter earnings call in August. "We will sell so much more of so many less products at a much higher full retail price."
Siegel said that when a retailer acknowledges it wants to shrink revenue, the goal is to regain pricing power.
A clearance rack in a Lululemon store in New York, Oct. 7, 2026.
Lululemon grew its sales by more than $500 million from fiscal 2024 to 2025. Yet its operating profit fell by about $300 million in the same time span. Shares are down around 65% over the past two years.
"Selling fewer options is not the same as selling fewer things," said Siegel. "Lulu has a long way to go, and simply cutting SKUs, simply saying we need to have better product is not the answer."
Siegel said too much of even the best product can dilute the value of a brand.
Lululemon reported $6.3 billion in U.S. sales in fiscal 2025. Siegel said $3 billion to $4 billion in domestic revenue is where companies typically hit a healthy saturation level.
"It&#x27;s a level where they can be large and still cool. Above that, they start cheapening what they stand for, literally cheapening the product, but also cheapening the perception," said Siegel.
He noted that Nike is an exception to this rule, after posting $20 billion in North American sales in fiscal 2026.
Still, the apparel and footwear giant, which has seen shares crater around 45% this year, said it is "rebalancing" its portfolio, having reduced revenue from classic footwear franchises by more than $2 billion in fiscal 2026, according to its fiscal 2026 fourth-quarter earnings call in June.
A sale sign is displayed on the soft drinks aisle at a Dollar General discount store in Inglewood, California, Sept. 29, 2026.
For small-box stores such as Dollar General and big-box retailers like BJ&#x27;s, cutting down the assortment doesn&#x27;t necessarily give them the ability to raise prices.
Rather, for those stores carrying thousands of brands, removing certain products can help them better manage inventory and refine their offerings to help stabilize the business.
"When you shrink a box ... and say, I&#x27;m going to focus more on curation, you&#x27;re getting smaller, but you&#x27;re trying to reestablish why someone&#x27;s walking into your box in the first place. And so it does get better, but it doesn&#x27;t necessarily help your bottom line as much," said Siegel.
BJ&#x27;s CEO Robert Eddy said reducing choice, such as cutting the number of scents of body wash, pushes sales into the remaining products on shelves and then makes room for more product categories that weren&#x27;t previously offered.
"That is sourcing sales growth as well and sort of giving us the formula where we can cut SKUs, and see sales go up, and see margin dollars go up," Eddy said on the company&#x27;s fiscal 2026 second-quarter earnings call in August.
A BJ&#x27;s Wholesale Club store in Miami, Florida, Nov. 21, 2025.
In June 2025, Dollar General said the elimination of 1,000 SKUs from the previous year opened up more shelf space for its best-selling products and added to its top line. By March 2026, the company said, it had reduced up to 1,500 SKUs, providing benefits to the overall supply chain.
"Being more productive there means getting product to the shelf faster and being there for the consumer with the right amount of items and products that she&#x27;s looking for as quickly as we possibly can," Dollar General CEO Todd Vasos said in the company&#x27;s fiscal 2026 second-quarter earnings call in August. "As we go forward, the team is looking at continued SKU rationalization, albeit probably more surgical in nature."
Successfully slashing products from shelves is difficult in practice.
Box stores risk losing customers to competitors that offer the products they scrapped. BJ&#x27;s, for instance, acknowledged that a previous attempt to cut SKUs was unsuccessful.
"We just cut SKUs which cut sales, and then we added some SKUs back and so really what we&#x27;re doing now is removing unnecessary choice," Eddy said on the company&#x27;s earnings call in August. "Think about in traditional soda, we don&#x27;t carry cans and one-liters and two-liters of the same product anymore."
Meanwhile, publicly traded brands that acknowledge sales need to decline in order for the business to grow still have answers to investors in the near term.
"It&#x27;s hard for a company ever to say we need to shrink anything, but it&#x27;s dramatically harder to say we need to shrink revenues," said Siegel. "Frequently we find that brands hit a peak, a healthy peak, extend past the peak by forcing it and then find their way back down pretty harshly."

### 53. ★★☆☆☆ Candidate named Hitler Mussolini elected mayor in Peru

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T12:00:10+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Benito Mussolini with Adolf Hitler at a meeting in Florence, Italy, in 1940. Photograph: Everett Collection Historical/Alamy
Benito Mussolini with Adolf Hitler at a meeting in Florence, Italy, in 1940. Photograph: Everett Collection Historical/Alamy
Candidate named Hitler Mussolini elected mayor in Peru
Leftwing Hitler Mussolini Simeon Flores beats far-right candidate in election in remote district of Andes
A businessman called Hitler Mussolini Simeon Flores has been elected the mayor of a remote district in the Peruvian Andes, according to local election data.
The 45-year-old, who shares two of his names with the notorious European dictators, won the race for mayor of Queropalca, in Peru’s central Huánuco region, and will take office on 1 January.
The candidate from the leftwing Ahora Nación party won 229 out of a total of 663 votes in elections on 4 October, according to the national office of electoral processes.
His closest rival, the far-right candidate Gaspar Osorio, received 204 votes.
Flores, who works in freight transportation, had belonged to Popular Force, the party of Peru’s rightwing president, Keiko Fujimori, until 2025, according to electoral authorities.
Despite the name being associated with Germany’s Nazi leader Adolf Hitler, several candidates with the name have won elections in Peru.
In 2021, the doctor Hitler Saavedra Casternoque was elected to parliament for the centrist party Somos Peru.
In 2018, Hitler Alba Sánchez was voted in as mayor of the Andean district of Yungar, saying during his campaign that he was “the good Hitler”.
