### 55. ★★☆☆☆ How a series of miscalculations restarted Saudi Arabia’s war in Yemen - CNN

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T09:14:02+00:00
- **发生了什么**: A pro-Saudi-backed Yemeni government fighter fires a vehicle-mounted weapon during what government forces say was an advance against the Iran-backed Houthis, near Dhubab, Taiz Governorate, Yemen, in this still image taken from video released on Monday.
Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman wanted to make his kingdom a magnet for investment , tourism and the world’s biggest sporting events. Now drones and missiles launched from Yemen are slamming into Saudi cities, drawing the kingdom deeper into the regional turmoil he hoped to leave behind.
The civil war in Yemen erupted once again this summer and significantly intensified last month, breaking years of relative calm. Saudi Arabia has for 11 years tried to contain the conflict on its southern border through military force and diplomacy – first waging war with the Iran-allied Houthi group, then pursuing peace talks with them and restoring diplomatic talks with their backer, Iran.
Despite the efforts, a series of miscalculations has left Riyadh exposed to further conflict in Yemen, experts say. Saudi Arabia’s ended its longstanding partnership with the UAE late last year over its backing of a rival force in Yemen, misjudged President Donald Trump’s willingness to back it if it needed support, and struggled to organize disintegrated armed factions fighting the Houthis in Yemen.
Now, Riyadh is trying to regain its footing by supporting its Yemeni partners in yet another campaign against the Houthis, an operation named “Yemen’s Dawn.”
Prince Mohammed’s ambitions to transform his kingdom have already faced several challenges since his rise to power more than a decade ago. Today, they’re coming under further strain as multi-front attacks on oil infrastructure cut into exports, major projects are canceled, and the kingdom is pulled into a war with no clear way out.
At least three people, including a Saudi pilot, were killed this week after Houthi forces struck several airports in the kingdom, according to Saudi Arabia. Regional turmoil has forced the suspension of star-studded film festivals, award shows, and music concerts this year. Meanwhile, the kingdom’s flagship financial conference, which is expected to draw high-profile business leaders at the end of October, is coming under pressure amid the strikes.
“They just needed the conflict to stay (as) far away from them as possible without the Houthis disrupting their transformation plans,” Michael Ratney, the former US ambassador to Saudi Arabia, told CNN. “Now they find themselves in the middle of it.”
Damaged military vehicles, reportedly sent by the United Arab Emirates to support Southern Transitional Council (STC) separatist forces, following an air strike carried out by the Saudi-led coalition in the port of Mukalla, southern Yemen, on December 30.
When Saudi warplanes struck a shipment of military vehicles sent to Yemen by the UAE last year, a decade-old alliance between two nations fighting Iran’s influence in Yemen shattered, leaving the kingdom to contend with the crisis alone after Abu Dhabi withdrew its forces. The break with the UAE shocked the region. Saudi Arabia launched a rare media campaign against its neighbor, accusing it of fueling instability in Sudan and Yemen. Saudi commentators publicly cast the UAE as a younger sibling rebelling against a wiser one.
Saudi Arabia is the “head and body” on which regional stability rests, wrote prominent Saudi journalist Dawood Al-Shirian in January. “If (Saudi Arabia) stumbles, the entire system is disrupted and the Gulf scatters.”
As Riyadh pressed for dominance in the Gulf, the ground shifted in Yemen. The four-year ceasefire with the Houthis was already fragile, and the rapid UAE withdrawal as demanded by Riyadh left thousands of fighters who had spent a decade under Abu Dhabi’s command disoriented by Riyadh’s strategy.
Fighters allied with the UAE say Abu Dhabi’s departure left a power vacuum the Saudis didn’t fill.
“There wasn’t a proper transition when the Emiratis left,” a southern Yemeni official, who asked to remain anonymous to speak freely to CNN, said. “It was frightening how different things became. The Emiratis’ command and control was very efficient, and the Saudis did not replace this efficiency.”
CNN reached out to the Saudi government for comment on whether the forces fighting the Houthis on the ground received enough support and whether there was a proper transition.
Protesters brandish billboards depicting the late Iranian Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei, flags of Yemen and Iran, and weapons as they take part in a rally held to condemn the US-Israel attacks on Iran that killed Khamenei, in Sana'a, Yemen, on March 1.
Eight weeks after the UAE withdrawal, the US and Israel launched a war on Iran, killing supreme leader Ayatollah Ali Khamenei. The moves sparked a conflict that has plunged the region into unprecedented turmoil and placed Washington’s allies, including Saudi Arabia, in Iran’s crosshairs.
The timing was unfortunate for Saudi Arabia, several experts say. After contending with the UAE-backed separatist push in Yemen, the US and Israel triggered a regional war. And despite years of trying to stop the war from breaking out in Yemen, the pressure Trump imposed on Iran was expected to eventually trigger a Houthi push on the kingdom.
“The Saudis had to respond after being attacked by an enemy force on their territory… so there was always going to be a response,” Ratney, the former US ambassador, said.
Even as Iranian missiles struck the kingdom from the east, Saudi attention focused on the south. The Houthis, still formally in a truce with Riyadh, appeared unwilling to aid their Iranian partners. Tehran instead drew the Saudis in.
In July, Iran sent a commercial plane to carry a Houthi delegation to Khamenei’s funeral in Tehran, defying a Saudi air blockade of Yemen. Riyadh struck the Houthi-held airport in Sanaa to prevent the plane from landing. The rebel group retaliated by declaring the truce dead and announced a naval blockade on Saudi ships. It also started a lightning offensive down Yemen’s coast, seizing strategic territory at the Bab al-Mandeb Strait in the largest escalation since 2022.
A satellite image shows the Saudi Arabia East-West pipeline, located across the Arabian Peninsula, after a strike on September 11.
The Houthis attacked Saudi oil facilities and pipelines, disrupting crude supplies and sending prices sharply higher. Saudi forces answered with hundreds of airstrikes.
“The Saudis have made several mistakes and failures,” Ahmed Nagi, a senior analyst for Yemen with the International Crisis Group think tank, told CNN, noting that Riyadh hadn’t prepared for a Houthi offensive and lacked coordination with allied Yemeni forces it inherited from the UAE.
“The areas they’ve lost created morale issues with their allies and now it’s a big challenge for the Saudis as the Houthis have more advanced weaponry which managed to reach several cities in Saudi Arabia,” he said.
US President Donald J. Trump and Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman attend a bilateral meeting at the Saudi Royal Court in Riyadh, on May 13, 2025.
Another miscalculation by Saudi Arabia was its reliance on continued American support, experts say. Despite signing a new defense pact with Washington last year and being designated a Major Non-NATO Ally by the US, Riyadh has been disappointed by what it sees as waning US commitment to its security.
Prince Mohammed spoke twice with Trump last month, urging him to strike the Houthis, sources told CNN at the time, but Trump declined to intervene. Washington instead met the Houthis in Oman, seeking to protect its own ceasefire with the group, which made it clear that its war was with Saudi Arabia alone, thus allowing US vessels to traverse the Red Sea freely.
For a military that has built its “entire worldview” around partnering with the US, the Saudis are realizing how unpredictable Trump is, said Ratney.
“It was never their concept or the structure of their military to fight these wars by themselves,” he said. “Under a normal US presidency, if something like this happens, we would’ve helped our partner in what would’ve been a rational and considered process.
“Now we don’t have that process, and it ultimately comes down to: what does Trump think? And the Saudis probably feel exposed as a result,” he added.
Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman, left, met Egyptian President Abdel Fattah el-Sisi, in Cairo, Egypt, on September 15.
Saudi Arabia has since looked elsewhere. Last month, Prince Mohammed flew to Egypt to meet President Abdel Fattah el-Sisi, then invited UAE Vice President Sheik Mansour bin Zayed to Riyadh a few weeks later to discuss regional developments. France agreed to deploy defense assets, including military personnel. Britain said it would send an aircraft to help refuel Saudi jets. More than 100 American military advisers are on the ground providing intelligence and targeting support under a newly established joint forces command.
Saudi Arabia, Turkey and Pakistan agreed on Monday to “activate collective deterrence measures” under the Mecca Joint Defense Agreement after an emergency meeting in Riyadh. The pact, set up earlier this year, is meant to coordinate defense and security cooperation among the three. Forces loyal to the UAE also appear to be taking part in the offensive, a sign of at least a partial thaw between the kingdom and the UAE.
Forces loyal to Yemen's Saudi-backed government ride in pick-up trucks along a road in the Hays district, south of Hodeidah on September 5.
But experts warn that outside support and early gains by the Saudi-backed Yemeni government will not settle the conflict. Infighting remains common among the factions, and there is still no clear political end state.
A sustained push against Iranian-backed forces, analysts say, would require organization, regional backing, funding, and logistics that the current coalition has not yet shown it can sustain.
“They need to unify their command centers, they need to resolve the issues they have between the various factions within the group,” Hisham Al Omeisy, a senior Yemen adviser for the European Institute of Peace told CNN’s Becky Anderson. “Territorial gains they’ve made would mean nothing if the (Saudi-backed) Yemeni government cannot maintain control over those areas.”
Many experts said however that the fighting in Yemen is connected to the US war in Iran, and as long as the conflict between Washington and Tehran is ongoing, the Iranians will continue having a powerful incentive to watch the Houthis disrupt Saudi Arabia.
Gulf states could manage to secure a separate diplomatic solution with Iran, analysts say, or escalate their military strikes to eliminate Houthi leadership.
“Neither of those scenarios fix the conflict in Yemen in any near term,” Ratney said. “The Saudis will have to just figure out an imperfect path ahead.”

### 56. ★★☆☆☆ Letter to the editor: Beware of right to natural gas question in Amendment 82 - SummitDaily.com

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Energy
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-09T20:51:07+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Letter to the editor: Beware of right to natural gas question in Amendment 82
The upcoming November Colorado state ballot will have a dangerous and deceptive initiative on it, Amendment 82, "Right to Natural Gas", which may seem harmless, but it's not. It's a blatant attempt by the fossil fuel industry to put barriers into our state constitution to make it difficult, if not impossible, to prevent developers and utilities from installing methane gas supplies to new developments. It's sparsely worded on purpose. A recent example of this is a development in Aurora with 25,000 homes, to which Xcel was requesting last year a natural gas supply line (5 miles long, costing $23 million), which would have caused higher bills for all Xcel customers to cover the cost, while allowing 25,000 additional homes to spew yet more carbon with equipment that will be obsolete in the near future.
At this point, every pound of carbon emitted matters, and the sooner we get off methane gas and other fossil fuels, and construct only all-electric buildings powered with renewable energy, the better. However, Amendment 82 is about more than that. It's about whether we should let monopoly utilities and corporate polluters write themselves into our constitution permanently. The reality is that renewables are less profitable than fossil fuels, not to us homeowners, but to traditional energy companies, which is why we can expect gas and big oil to fight this transition at every turn. No pollution in the constitution: Vote no on Amendment 82!

### 57. ★★☆☆☆ Tesla drops 'Full Self-Driving' brand name in Europe after regulator pushback

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-09T19:21:14+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Tesla drops &#x27;Full Self-Driving&#x27; name in Europe after regulator push
Tesla changed the brand name for its "Full Self-Driving (Supervised)," or FSD, systems to "Assisted Driving" in Europe under pressure from German regulators.
Elon Musk&#x27;s automaker markets the system for $99 per month under its old brand name in the U.S.
In its second-quarter earnings update, Tesla said it had 1.48 million "active" FSD subscriptions but did not say how many were subscribed via free trials.
A Tesla Model 3 is shown driving on the highway with FSD 14.2.2.3 self-driving supervised software in Irvine, California, U.S., Jan. 28, 2026.
Elon Musk &#x27;s automaker Tesla has changed the brand name of its "Full Self-Driving (Supervised)," or FSD, system in Europe to "Assisted Driving" after German regulators called the branding "somewhat misleading."
The package is still sold under the Full Self-Driving (Supervised) branding in the U.S. as a $99 per month subscription for Tesla owners.
Tesla is gunning for approvals across Europe, where EV sales have been on the rise this year amid soaring fuel costs caused by the war in Iran, and where Tesla sales have shown signs of recovery from a sales slump last year.
On Tuesday, the German Federal Ministry of Transport , known as the BMV, said in a statement that Tesla&#x27;s brand name "FSD" was "somewhat misleading," adding that "It is not a system that takes over the entire driving task. Rather, it is a driver assistance system that can support certain driving tasks such as accelerating, braking, and steering. The driver must remain attentive at all times."
Tesla has been pressuring automotive regulators in Europe, especially in the Netherlands, for rapid approval of their system. Reuters reported that in some cases, Tesla&#x27;s " browbeating " pushed regulators to reduce the intensity of their safety tests and reviews of the company&#x27;s technology.
The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration, the U.S. automotive safety regulator, is also investigating Tesla&#x27;s FSD to determine if it is safe for drivers to use in fog, glaring sun or other reduced visibility conditions, and is investigating Tesla to determine whether FSD caused Tesla drivers to commit traffic violations — some that resulted in collisions.
Tesla drops &#x27;Full Self-Driving&#x27; brand name in Europe after regulator pushback
Verizon, T-Mobile and Verizon fall as SpaceX U.S. network plans whack telcos
U.S. suspends Microsoft, Adobe from green card labor program in foreign worker crackdown
Amazon overhauls aging devices lineup with higher-priced Alexa tablet, dumping the budget Fire
In December 2025, a judge in California determined that Tesla had engaged in deceptive marketing around its Autopilot and FSD systems, falsely implying they were fully automated driving systems. Tesla has countersued the California DMV to try to shed that "false advertiser" label in a Los Angeles superior court. That litigation is ongoing.
Autonomous systems expert Missy Cummings, an engineering professor at George Mason University and outspoken critic of Tesla and other autonomous vehicle developers, said renaming FSD isn&#x27;t a strong enough measure to ensure the systems are safe for broad release in Europe.
In an email to CNBC, Cummings said European agencies are "ignoring both past and ongoing NHTSA investigations" into safety defects with Tesla&#x27;s FSD.
"The EU is struggling to keep up in the AI marketplace," she said. "Musk sees this and is definitely bullying them as a result."
She criticized the Dutch regulator, known as RDW, for approving Tesla FSD for release in Europe, "based on secretive data," adding that, "The public should demand transparency in any approval of a transportation system with embedded AI."
Tesla and RDW did not immediately respond to a request for comment.
In its second-quarter earnings update, Tesla said it had 1.48 million "active" FSD subscriptions but did not say how many were activated from free trials or other incentives.

"its annualized revenue was nearing $50 billion at the end of September"
Annualized revenue is a way to estimate how much money a company will earn in a year based on its current sales over a shorter period. It helps investors understand the company's consistent earning power, kind of like projecting your monthly earnings to see what you might make in a full year.
"announcing a $150 billion share buyback authorization"
A share buyback authorization is a formal approval that lets a company repurchase its own outstanding shares up to a specified amount or within a set time frame. Investors care because buying back shares reduces the number of shares available, which can raise earnings per share and concentrate ownership—similar to a company collecting and destroying extra tickets to increase the value of the remaining ones—and it signals how management prefers to deploy cash.

VANCOUVER, BC , Oct. 10, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Stock Preachers News Commentary  - Semiconductor equities began October on firm footing, with the iShares Semiconductor ETF advancing for five consecutive sessions and the sector's largest company reaching a record high, before retreating through the week ended October 9. Sentiment shifted on Thursday, when the Financial Times reported that OpenAI had told investors its annualized revenue was nearing $50 billion at the end of September, below the approximately $70 billion figure that had been cited in media reports. Because semiconductor valuations are closely tied to expectations for AI infrastructure spending, the report weighed on the group, and the PHLX Semiconductor Index finished the week lower while the broader market was more resilient. Active Companies from around the markets with current developments this week include: Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company Limited (NYSE: TSM ), NVIDIA Corporation (NASDAQ: NVDA ), Intel Corporation (NASDAQ: INTC ), Arm Holdings plc (NASDAQ: ARM ), Micron Technology, Inc. (NASDAQ: MU ).
The pullback follows a period of substantial gains. The PHLX Semiconductor Index was up 73% year to date as of late September, and several of the sector's largest constituents have recorded triple-digit gains in 2026. Higher long-term Treasury yields and a rise in crude oil prices added pressure midweek, as higher discount rates tend to weigh most on companies valued on long-term growth expectations.
Company-specific developments shaped performance within the group. Two companies reported record milestones early in the week, while others faced open questions regarding a manufacturing partnership, ongoing litigation and the sustainability of AI-related demand.
CONTINUED... Read the full weekly market analysis in The Roadmap at: stockpreachers.com
In other industry developments and happenings in the market this week include:
Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company Limited (NYSE: TSM )
TSMC shares reached a record high on October 5 after Elon Musk confirmed early-stage discussions about a role for the company in his planned Terafab chip complex in Texas , calling them "just discussions. " On October 8 the company reported  September revenue of NT $511.86 billion , up 54.6% from a year earlier and down 0.6% from August, bringing revenue for the first nine months of 2026 to NT $3.90 trillion , up 41.1% .
NVIDIA  shares reached a record high on October 5, bringing their year-to-date gain to approximately 24.8% and their advance since the August 26 earnings report to approximately 14% . The company has also shown relative strength during recent sector weakness: on September 28, when semiconductor shares declined on AI safety concerns, NVIDIA advanced after announcing a $150 billion share buyback authorization and releasing two open-source tools designed to help control AI agents.
Intel  shares declined on October 5 after Musk indicated that TSMC could participate in Terafab, a project Intel joined as a partner in April. On October 8 Musk said his companies will build and operate the Texas complex and that another chipmaker could at most sublease part of the site. Intel has said it will remain involved, although the scope of its role has not been finalized. Despite the decline, Intel shares were up approximately 215% year to date as of the October 5 close.
Arm  shares declined during a five-day jury trial in federal court in Wilmington, Delaware , where Qualcomm alleges that Arm breached their licensing agreements and sought to undermine Qualcomm's dealings with Meta Platforms. Arm denies the claims. The case went to the jury on October 9, and the judge has not yet ruled on whether a clause that could let Qualcomm stop paying royalties for five years is enforceable. Arm reports earnings on November 4.
Micron  shares declined alongside other memory producers despite Rosenblatt raising its price target to $1,900 from $1,500 on October 6, citing a beat-and-raise quarter and the potential for buybacks. Memory producers had advanced with the broader group in early October before sentiment shifted later in the week.
